=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.98)
DRAFT
ALTERNATIVE COURSES
(1) In view of the strong desire of the Navy and the
Army that an immediate agreement be reached between the
Department of State and the Treasury Department in regard to
the rate of exchange for the Outlying Japanese Islands a
meeting might be held between a representative of the State
Department and a representative of the Treasury Department
at a very high level (for example, between the Under
Secretaries of the two Departments). On that occasion the
representative of the State Department might endeavor to
urge agreement on the basis of a ten cent rate for the
Outlying Islande and Formosa. Failing agreement on suoh a
rate a compromise might be sought on one of the following
l1nee:
(a) An eight cent rate for the Outlying Islands,
without prejudice to some other rate for the Out-
lying Ialands and for Formosa in common, to be
determined when the time comes for fixing a rate
for Formosa.
(b) A five cent rate for the Outlying Islands on
the underetanding now that the common rate for
both Formosa and the Outlying Islands will be ten
cents when the time arrives for fixing the rate
for Formosa.
(0)

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
(o) A compromise rate for both Formosa and the
Outlying Islands at eight cents.
Alternatively,
(2) We might inform the Navy Department orally that in
response to the Navy's request for advice as to rate of ex-
change for the Outlying Islands we have expressed our views
fully and we feel that no useful purpose is likely to be
served by our endeavoring to argue our case any further with
the Treasury Department. It would be explained further to
the Navy that our position thus stated refers only to the
question of a rate for the Outlying Islands and we will
expect to be consulted further when the time comes for con-
sidering the rate for Formosa, which involves political
considerations of great importance.
With regard to the second alternative, in as much as we
have an inescapable responsibility from the point of view of
international bearings on this question it seems doubtful
that adoption of the second alternative would best further
the interests and responsibilities of the Department except
as a last resort following failure to effect any satisfactory
solution through a compromise.
It is therefore recommended that we adopt at least
initially the first alternative.

STANDARD FORM NO. 64
Office Memorandum
UNITED.SPAS GOVERNMENT
THE SECRETARY
TO
The Secretary
DATE:
FROM :
A-C Mr. Thorp
SUBJECT:
Japanese Rate of Exchange for Troop Pay.
be adjueted laten
Mr. McCloy must know at once the rate of exchange to be
used for the purpose of paying our troops in Japan. He called
Mr. Grew and Mr. Grew requested me to present the problem to
894.5151/8-1545
you. Unfortunately, Treasury and State do not agree on the
proper rate.
The prewar rate of exchange was 23 cents per yen. No one
has any informed judgment as to what the general rate of ex-
change will be when Japan resumes a more normal foreign trade.
Therefore, it is likely that there will be two rates of exchange,
one fixed for the purposes of troop pay and military accounting,
the other the general commercial rate.
The Treasury Department has recommended that the troop pay
rate be fixed at five cents, or twenty yen to the dollar. The
Treasury Department's attitude stems from the criticism which
has been directed at certain rates of troop pay exchange in
Europe and the troubles created when the troop pay exchange rate
is above the general rate. It believes that the proposed rate
is sufficiently low to avoid any such complaints.
The State Department has recommended that the troop pay
rate be fixed at ten cents, or ten yen to the dollar. The
State Department's position is based on the proposition that
the troop pay rate should approximate the general rate, or else
one is using an artificial exchange rate as a method of levying
CS/A
upon the occupied country, in fact, a kind of war bounty or
looting. Since it would be virtually impossible to raise the
rate used in troop pay (which is the equivalent of giving each
soldier fewer yen), but it could easily be lowered, we have
urged the higher rate, subject to downward adjustment from time
to time as may seem appropriate.
894.5151/5-1545
This is really not an economic problem. From the point
of view of troop morale, there is no doubt but that the five
cent rate would be best. On political grounds, relating to
the fair treatment of the Japanese, the ten cent rate, subject
to later adjustment, would seem more desirable. If the State
Department wishes to state to the War Department that, for
political reasons, it desires a ten cent rate, the Treasury
will yield. Otherwise, I think we should concede the point.
Mr. Ballantine and Mr. Grew are the experts on the political
aspects of Japan.
Lis
A-C:WLThorp:eh

DEPARTMENT )I SIATE
With reference to the note verbale no. 12877
of October 29th the Italian Embassy points out that it
has received confirmation, through information furnished
to the Italian Ministry of Foreign Affairs by the Swedish
Legation in Tokyo, that among the Italian funds in Japan
are included 2l3 kilograms of gold bouillon deposited at the
Bank of Japan and belonging to the Bank of Italy. This
gold has been blocked by the United States military autho-
rities in that country.
The Italian Embassy in Washington has been re-
894.5151/9-1545
quested to intervene with the competent authorities in
Washington in order to have this gold included in the Ita-
lian assets in Japan of which the unblocking has been re-
quested. Similar steps have been taken already by the
Swedish Legation with the American Military Headquarters
in Tokyo.
The Italian Embassy in Washington shall be grate-
ful for a prompt consideration of the above request and

My dear Mr. Dowling:
You will recall our conversation of a few days ago regarding
-412146
the unblocking of funds pertaining to the Italian Government and
ment of State on this question with notes No.l0603 of September 2 and
subsequent. We have now received another telegram from Signor Indelli,
former Ambassador to Tokyo. You will see from the text of the telegram
which I am enclosing that the situation of the Italian officials in
894.5151/9-1545
Japan is becoming more and more distressing and there is an urgent need
of the unblocking of the funds pertaining to them.
May I recall to you that on this question some assurances
had already been given sometime ago by the Department on the fact that
instructions had been sent to the American military authorities in Japan
in order to obtain that a liberal licensing policy be adopted in con-
nection with the aforesaid Italian funds?
I shall be grateful to you if, realizing the dire need of the
Itelian nationals at present in Japan, you will kindly press the matter
with the American military authorities in that country in order to
obtain that a decision be reached on the subject.
Thanking you for your kind attention to this matter, I am

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
It is understood that the Italian Ambassador wishes to take up
with you the possibility of a provisional peace, or an early revi-
sion of the armistice terms as mentioned by Mr. Byrnes in his press
interview of August 22. The Ambassador will argue that, in view of
the likelihood that the final peace settlement with Italy may be
delayed, a conorete gesture of good will on the part of Britain and
the United States is needed now. (Di Stefano tells me seoretly
that De : asperi was perhaps too enthusiastic about Tarchiani's re-
ports of nis recent interviews with Mr. Byrnes and the President,
and that now Communist elements in the Italian Government are argu-
ing that these American assurances were meaningless.) The British
are now considering our proposal for an armistice revision (see at-
tached copies of the Secretary's telegram to you of September 24
894.5151/9-2945
and the memorandum on armistice revision approved by the President
on June 30) which we feel more than ever necessary on the basis of
reports out of London on the Italian peace treaty negotiations, as
well as reports from Italy of dwindling Anglo-American prestige and
mounting agitation against the Parri Government.
Tarohiani will also ask whether the publication of President
Roosevelt's letter to Mr. Armour portends any further aotion in re-
spect to the Spanish regime, as the Italians wvish to follow our
lead. He will probably enquire as well regarding our attitude
towards Argentina.

894.5151/9-2945
DIVISION OF FINANCIAL AFFAIRS
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
EW
The Italian Ambassador called on me this morning
to ask what, if anything, could be done toward the
unfreezing of Italian Government and private funds in
Japan and handed me the attached copy of a memorandum
in regard to this subject. He pointed out that the
Italian diplomats who had been interned for the past
two years by the Japanese Government had now been
XR
freed but were in need of funds and he hoped some
assistance could be made available to them.
The Ambassador also brought up the subject of
relations between France and Italy (memorandum attached)
saying that de Gasperi had had a most satisfactory con-
versation with General de Gaulle in Paris a few days
ago and that he wished us to know that Italy was pre-
pared to go a long way in reestablishing close rela-
tions with France. He felt that this was necessary
for the development of France as well as of Italy.
8
The Ambassador then took up the question of a
possible revision of the Italian Armistice terms inasmuch
as it seemed there would be some delay in reaching agree-
151/9-2945
ment at London on the terms of the Italian peace treaty.
He pointed out that the extreme Left ahements in Italy
B 12 1919
ED
were
BU
0at
Di8t.

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
were now arguing that developments at London showed
the need for a more friendly attitude on the part of
Italy toward Russia and were saying that if Italy
would look more to Russia for guidance the peace prob-
lems could be easily settled. The Ambassador felt that
some concrete gesture on the part of the British and
American Governments would go far to counteract this
propaganda. I assured him that this matter was receiv-
ing sympathetic consideration and referred to the
Secretary's press conference statements on the subjeot
prior to his departure for London.
The Ambassador then took up the question of the
civil supply program for Italy saying that he was con-
cerned at the possibility of an interruption since the
FEA funds for Italy would shortly be exhausted while
UNRRA funds had not yet been appropriated. I told him
that I knew of this situation but did not believe there
would be any interruption in the supply program. The
Ambassador said he was most grateful for this informa-
tion.
Dean Acheson

ROYAL ITALIAN EMBASSY
WASHINGTON, D. C.
DEPARTMENT OFS
No. 10603
SEP 2 9 1945
2
MEMORANDUM
The Italian Embassy has learned that orders have
been issued in Japan for the freezing of government and
private Italian funds in that country.
Such orders seem not to take into account the
Italian declaration of war against Japan, and deeply af-
fect Italian interests in that country. Moreover, from a
894.5151/9-2945
cable received from Tokyo sent by the Italian representa-
tives there, it appears that all steps for the repatriation
and assistance of Italians already interned by the Japanese
Government are greatly jeopardized by the measures in ques-
tion.
The Itslian Embassy will appreciate it if instruc-
tions will urgently be sent with the view of obtaining that
the competent military American authorities in Tokyo take in-
to consideration the possibility of the adoption of equitable
CS/LE
measures on the matter.
Department of State
Washington D. C.
APR291946

September 29, 1945.

I. It appears that JK has also been plagued by the problem of American
and other firms which sell servicus, such es news and motion piatures, in....
Japan, accepting payment in blocked yen. Although notice has been served
that yen acquired in this manner would not be transferrable into dollars,
the firms in question have never the less attempted to transfer them in one
way or another, and further efforts in this direction are anticipated.
2.  has accordingly decided that the only way to keep this problem
from beconing unmanageable is to choke it off at the outset by prohibiting
m
the sale of any services in Japan, except those approved by SCip which are
to be paid for in dollars. They have proposed this solution in the attached
panor which is to be circulated to Yar and Treasury Departments.
3. Before sending it out they inguired whether the adoption of this
policy would in any way embarass GA. Mr. deWilde and I informed them that
Ga is entirely in accord with their views and would in no way be embarassed.
We pointed out also that the blocked mark balances heve already begun to
accumulate, and that there might be some difficulty in the case of Germany
in securing air-tight enforcement of the policy they recommend. To this
they replied that they werc faced with the same situation, and that things
had probably gone no further in Garmany then they have in Japan. It is to
be noted that we are making a suggestion almost identical with theirs in a
cable we have drafted for transmission by War Department to OMGUs on
admission of U. S. business firms in Germany.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
My dear Mr. Clayton:
Reference is made to the memorandum of the Department of
State to the Secretary of the Treasury dated April 5, enclosing a
copy of a note from the French Embassy relative to the yen account
of the French Government with the Tokyo Branch of the Bank of Indo-
China. The note requests that "in view of the inability of the
French Government to send funds to Japan" the Department of State
"intervene with the competent Federal authorities" in order to
obtain the release of 250,000 yen per month.
This Department is prepared to authorize, in so far as
894.5151/5-2146
the Trading with the enemy Act is concerned, the remittance of any
finds which the French Government may desire to send to Tokyo for
the functioning of the office of the Consulate General of France in
Yokohama. It would appear that the recently established remittance
facilities, under which dollars are paid to the War Department for
deposit to the SCAP account with the Secretary of the Treasury and
outpayment in yen at the military rate of conversion is made by the
Bank of Japan, could be utilized for this purpose.
The apparent inability of the French Government to draw
upon its funds on deposit in Japan is not caused by reason of the
impact of any regulations administered by the Treasury Department,
but would appear to result from the fact that pursuant to SCAP
directives to the Japanese Government, the Tolyo Branch of the Bank
of Indo-China has been closed. In addition property in Japan belong-
ing to persons outside of Japan was ordered blocked. It is under-
stood that a radio has been dispatched to SCAP requesting information
on the size of the French Government accounts in the Tokyo Branch of
the Bank of Indo-China and on the status of the bank.
Should the reply to this radio indicate that the Bank of
Indo-China is solvent, and should the French Government not desire to
remit new funds to Japan for the operations of the Consulate General,
it is suggested that SCAP be authorized to effect the release of the
requested sums. This Department perceives no objection to permitting

ESTIE B. Moss
For some time we and all the missionary agoncies
Director of Promation
working in Japan and Korea have been concerned about the
rate of exchange which has been fixed at 15 to l. We
realize that this rate of exchange has a bearing on the
problem of inflation in these two countries and that
the whole question is one of considerable complexity.
At the same time, the hardships resulting from this rate of
exchange, both upon our porsonnel in these two countries
and also upon our relief operations, are very great and so
consideration of the possibility of a change in the rate
of exchange seems necessary.
lWhile at the present time we are not disposed to
ask for any special revileges, although that may be necessary
C
in the future, I am writing simply to ask that if any chango
7
in the established rate of erchange should be contemplated,
A1
the interests of the missionary and relief agencies must be
included.

FROM : JK - Mr. Whitman
SUBJECT: PrOposed Cable to SCAP
The
AFrench have approached Treasury for unblocking from Banque de 1'Ando
Chine account with the Yokohama Specie Bank of three and one-half
million yen, based on estimates of two and one half million yen for
support during the first quarter of 1947, of the French Mission in Japan;
and one million yen for support of French eleemosynary institutions in
Japan for the year 1947. While the French will not press for the first
item, feeling that free yen can be obtained from SCap, they are anxious
to hesp their charitable and religious groups, which are now short of
funds. JK and Treasury will not object to unblocking of the one million
yen item,if it can be accomplished by freeing only funds in the account
which did not originate in collaborationist activities.
At present the French are conferring with JK for release of certain
pre-mar Indo-Chinese assets in Japan. In this connection it would estab-
lish a valuable precedent for SCap to distinguish between the clean and
tainted portions of the account and strengthen the possfble State posi-
the French to seek the value of the material aid to Japan during thewer in tle
form of a reparations claim.
DRAFT CABLE TO SCAP
Reourad War 87591, 12 December 1946.
1. French Govt has requested unblocking of one million
yen from Banque de 1'Indo Chine account with YSB for 1947
support of French charitable institutions in Japan.
State and Treasury favor unblocking in your discretion of
12-2646
sum in question, if it can be made available from any portion
of account which did not originate from Indo Chinese activities
in support of the Japan war effort. Request reply soonest to
permit answer to French.

=== Page 86 === (confidence 0.99)
Missions Confo
of North America
I56 FIFTH AVENUE, NEW YORK 10, N. Y.
Fobruary 4, 1947
The following action was taken by the Foreign Missions Conferenee
of Morth America at its Fifty-third Annual Meeting at Buok Mill Falls,
on January 17, 1947:
Because we believe that there is mo more important factor
in the present attempt to democratise and rehabilitate the Japanese
nation than the return of Christiam missionaries and the re-establiskmens
of Christian institutions, and
Because the return of missionaries and the re-establishment
of Christian institutions is being seriously impeded by the present
unrealistic yen-dollar exchange rate of fifteen to one, and
Being assured that an adjustment in the rate of exohange would
not have a bad effect upon the internal economy of Japan and not
deter the recovery of Japan's legitimate economic life,
The Foreign Missions Conference of North America in anmal
messiom at Buck Hill Falls, Pa., January 17, 1947, requests the
Secretary of State of the United States of Ameriea to use his influemee
to effect an equitable and realistie rate of yen-dollar exchange,
and asks that the officers of the Foreign Missions Conference of
North America communicate this request to the Department of State.

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.98)
TOP -SECRET
ENCLOSURE
REVISION OF YEN DOLLAR MILITARY CONVERSION RATE
Report by the
State-War-Navy Coordinating Subcommittee for the Far East
THE PROBLEM
1. To recommend the action to be taken on the proposal of
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers to change the present
military conversion rate of 15 yen equals 1 dollar to 75 yen
equals 1 dollar for both Japanese and Korean yen.
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
2. See ppendix "A".
DISCUSSION
3. See Appendix "B".
CONCLUSIONS
4. It is concluded that:
a. Change in the military conversion rate to the full
extent recuested by SCAP is not considered desirable. Some
8
results would arise therefrom, contrary to the anti-
2
Ainflation program of SCAP, and further a reasonable margin
2-
FEC.
should be preserved between the military conversion rate and
Deld
the level at which current estimates indicate the commercial
rate of exchange will ultimately be set.
b. The objectives cited by SCAP can in part be achieved
by a subsiay program to Japanese producers of Px supplies
and by casures to increase the importation of essential
items of household equipment from the United States.
c. The military conversion rate be set at 50 yen to 1
U.S. dollar for Japan and Korea.
Enclosure
-15-
SWNCC 343/1
TOP SECRET

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP BECRET
RECOMMENDATIONS
5. It 1e 1 commer.ded that:
a. SWNCC approve the above conclusions;
b. After approval by SWNCC, copies of the approved
paper be forwarded to the State, War and Navy Departments
for information, guidance and appropriate implementation;
c. The State Department be requested to transmit the
conclusions to the Treasury Department and the U. S.
representative, FEC, for their information;
d. The cable in Appendix "C" be dispatched by the JCS
to SCAP provided the JCS have no objection from a military
point of view.
e. No part of this document be released for publication
until authorized by SCAP.

APPENDIX "A"
FACTS BEARING ON THE PROBLEM
1. On 7 November 1946, SCAP tranem1tted a letter (Annex 
to Appendix "A") to the Chief of Staff United States Army reoom-
mending that the present military conversion rate be reset at 75
to 1 for both J-yanese and Korean yen. The present military
conversion rte was established by War Department radio W-50l30
dated 15 August 1945 (Annex "B" to Appendix "A"). JCS 1231/4
dated 8 September 1945 was issued as a matter of recording the
15 to 1 rate.
2. The reasons given by SCAP for changing the present mili-
tary conversion rate may be briefly stated as follows:
a. The 15 to 1 rate now in effect was set for planning
purposes for the initial phases of the occupation operations,
and was to be subject to revision depending on further
developments.
b. The present rate is not an official commercial rate
of exchange, but rather an index for translating dollar pay
into yen for occupation personnel.
c. Observed prices of commodities commonly purchased
by occur ion personnel have risen by roughly five times
since 5 August 1945. Thcrefore, if 15 to 1 rate was satis-
factory in August 1945, present rate should be 75 to 1.
d. It requires from 25 to 250 yen to purchase in Japan
what one dollar purchases in the United States.
e. A further rise in prices is anticipated in Japan.
f. Occupation personnel would not purchase more than
2% of total volume of Japanese sales even at 75 to 1 rate.
Therefore, change in rate would have little inflationary
effect on the Japanese economy.
g. Change in rate would have little effeot on commercial
agencies licensed to do business in Japan. Dollar remittances
of such agencies to Japan would yield 5 times as much yen as
at present, but in case certain yen remittances should be

would result from reduction in the dollar value

from the 15 to 1 rate which represents a very great over-

in the Japanese economy.

SUBJECT: Revision of Yen-Dollar Military Conversion Rate.
TO:
Chief of Staff, United States Army, War Department,
Washington 25, D. C.
1. It is recommended that the present military conversion
rate of 15 yen equal 1 dollar be reset at 75 yen equal 1 dollar
for both Japan and Korean yen on or about 1 January 1947. The
reascns therefor are set forth hereinafter.
2. The prosent military rate of conversion was set by War
Department : adio W50130, dated 17 August 1945, which states that
this rate is set for planning purposes for the initial phases of
operations and is subject to revision depending on further
developments ana information.
3. Because of the absence of commercial transactions be-
tween the United States and Japan during the war the present
military conversion rate is not an official commercial rate of
exchange based on the value of the yen in relation to dollars in
the world market. It should not be so considered or announoed.
Rather it is a purely military conversion rate more or less arbi-
trarily adopted in order to enable occupational personnel to make
legitimate and open market purchases or indigenous goods and
services at reasonable prices in areas where the yen is legal
tender. Based on the best information available at the time
the military conversion rate was set successively:
(a) At 20 yen equal 1 dollar by the Navy in the Marianas.
(b) At 10 yen equal 1 dollar prior to the Ryukyus campaigr
(c) At 15 yen equal 1 dollar for use in Japan and Korea.
It should be noted that this military conversion rate is merely
an index whereby a statutory rate of pay for military personnel
as stated in United States dollars can be converted to yen.

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.97)
DIOBET
Since the intridiotion of the Military Payment Certirs
on 19 July 1J46 the military conversion rate has become
the rate at which the Army will sell yen for dollars or doll
instruments with no re-purchase arrangements.
4.
The recommended military conversion rate of 75 yen equal
1 dollar 1s based on the observed rise during the year Auguet
1945 to August 1946 in th: cost of articles and services oommonly
necded by United Statcs nationals in Japan and Korea. During that
period, as indicated in Tables 1, 2 and 3 (Tabe "A", "B" and "C)
the cost of kitchen utenells has 1rcreased 895%; household
furnichings 63c%; indi enous 1tems such as films, cameras, silks,
pearls and binoculars 473%; and domestic servants 529%. A
weightcd index (Table 4)(Tab "D") of 1tems purcnased by occupa-
tional personnel shows that costs for bachelors have risen 479%
and for dependents 524%. Assuming tnat the rate of 15 yen equal
1 dollar war . atisfactory in August 1945 the equivalent rate
today choule be 5 times greater or 7s yen cqual 1 dollar.
5. A comparison of 16 standard household items shows that
 dollar spent in the Urited Statcs wii! purchase the equivalent
o1 from 25 to 25C yen spert in Janan (Table 5) (Tab "E").
6. It is considered that factors conducive to future price
increases in Japan outweigh those which might cause a reduction
in priccs. Table 6 (Tab "F") indicates that serious inflation
exists in Japan.
The rise in the cost of living between Deoember
1944 and Apr11 1946 was probably somewhere between l000% and
1800%. A careful cost analysis of the items purohased for the
Army Exchange Certral Purchasing Ofrice, for resale to occupa-
tional perscnnel through the Exchange system, reveals that the
cost of lubor is the principal factor contributing to the high
price of mor mandise in Japan. Wages have not yet increased pro-
portionately to prices. The demands of labor have been greatly
influenced by the cost of food supplies. While the current
favorable crop situation in Japan may tend to ease the pressure
on food prices there is no definite assurance that this improv
ment is permanent.

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.97)
BECBET
7. It 1s considered that the adoption of a 75 to
sion rate would not have a seriodd anrlationary effeot 
Japancse economy. Legitimate purdhasek in the civilian eoonem
by occupational forces currently average two million dollars
month. There cre indioations that a considerable amount of
indigenous goods are being purchased through barter transaotion
or with yen derived from the illegal sale of American goods to
Japanese nationals. Including both types of purchases and allow
ing for possible increased purchases resulting from the 75 to 1
conversion rate, it is unlikely under any circumstances that out
of a 12 million dollar monthly cash payroll the occupational
forces would spend more than 5 million dollars for Japanese goods
or services. At 75 to 1 this sum would constitute only 2% of the
total volume of monthly sales in Japan which are estimated at
about 18 billion yen. Furthermore with the more favorable rate
of exchange it is quite possible that there would be a great re-
duction in or elimination of illegal sales of American goods.
8. The adoption of the 75 to 1 conversion rate would entail
minor advantage. nd disadvantages for purely commercial agencies
licensed to o business in Japan, such as news services. For the
most part yen expenditures of these agencies are mado from yen
revenue. Transfer of dollars to yen for such expenditures are
negligible but when made under the 75 to 1 rate would realize
5 times as many yer as under the 15 to 1 rate. Excess yen are
held in banks pending establishment of a free rate of exchange.
On the other hand, should future operating conditions be changed
to permit certain agencies to pay yen into the Foreign Trade Fund
and receive dollars from the Export Fund to defray approved
dollar operating costs, it may be necessary for those agencies to
raise their prices somewhat in order to accumulate sufficient yen
if the 75 to 1 military conversion rate is used as the rate of
exchange for such reimbursements.
9. The present 15 to 1 rate works a serious and unjust hard-
ship on elee~ -ynary institutions dependent on contributions in
SWNCC 343/1
-22 -
Annex "A" to Appendix "

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.99)
BEO RET
dollars or other foreign currency, and on repatriates who
required to exchange their dollars or other foreign ourrenoy 
yen since they get only 20 cents actual worth for every dollar.
Recipients of retirement and pension checks in Korea are able to
purchase so little with the yen proceeds from those cheoks after
conversion that the Commanding General, XXIvth Corps, has recom-
mended that such checks be held in the Treasury until such time
as a more favcrable military conversion rate or a commercial rate
of exchange has been established.
10. It is considered that the adoption of the 75 to 1 mil1-
tary conversion rate is a matter of simple justice to United States
military and civilian personnel. It is not feasible to reduce
occupational personnel and their dependents to an existenae de
pendent solely on United States supplies and services. It is
inevitable that such personnel will purchase native items and
services. To the extent that when such purchases are made the
15 to 1 rate acts in effect as a reduction in pay. The Army Ex-
change Central Purchasing Office has been able to protect U.S.
personnel somewhat from unwarranted price increases imposed by
unscrupulous merchants. However, unless the conversion rate is
changed the future services rendered by the Post Exchange will be
uncertain since ne tendency will be more and more towards pur-
chases in te open market with illicitly acquired yen instead of
legitimate purchases in the Post Exchanges. Should the military
conversion rate be changed, it is felt that the Army Exchange
Central Purchasing Office will be able to minimize sympathetic
price rises by making costs analysis before signing contracts.
Furthermore, if the 75 to 1 rate is adopted the Army Exchange.
Central Purchasing Office, by making only moderately increased
price offers, will be able to stimulate the production of many
items which are now practically impossible to obtain because of
their high costs and consequent low demand.
11. The present 15 to 1 rate is contributory to moral re
laxations of United States personnel and therefore detrimental
to the maintenance of a proper state of discipline in the Armcd

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
Forces. Buch personnel feel that the United States Govern
sacriricing their intereste in favor of the Japanese and Koree
As a result they feel morally justified in either indulging in
barter transa-tions or purchasing yen at a more favorable rate
from black market racketeers. This attitude is not universal
but personnel who purchase their yen at military conversion rate
at finance offices feel that their moral integrity is being
preserved unjustly and at a great financial sacrifice.
12. The preceding discussion relating to the adoption of
75 to 1 military conversion rate is based primarily on conditions
in Japan. However, economic and other pertinent conditions are
almost identical in Korea. The change in rate should therefore
be adopted in both countries simultaneously.
13. Since any delay in revising the military conversion rate
prolongs a situation which is basically unfair to occupational
personnel and is of little advantage to the Japanese and Korean
people, it is  commended that the military conversion rate be
reset at 75  en equal 1 dollar effective on or about 1 January
1947. It is requested that this Headquarters be informed at the
earliest practicable date as to the decision in the matter.

APPENDIX "B"
DISCUSSION
1. It 1s apparent from the information furnished by
and from observations of persons returning from Japan, that th
15 to 1 rate represents a very considerable overvaluation of th
yen measured by yen price levels for indigenous consumers goods
and services in Japan and dollar prices in the United States.
Act on to adjust the purchasing power of orcupation personnel
in Japan: and Korea is belleved to be of derinite importance to
the morale : such American personnel and to the suocessful
accomplishment of the occupation.
2. Although the Supreme Commander's analysis inoludes
items normally procured by occupational personnel in the local
ecor.omy of Japan, his analysis dors not reveal actual amount
spent by representative occupation personnel on items which
statistics show have risen in price by approximately 500% in
apan nor the percentare of such expenditures to total living
costs of occupatior personnel in Japan. It is understood that
s'me hourehold cquipment required for initial furnishing of
occupatin force billcts is supplicd by the Army, and this
together with custodial servants is paid for in dollar rentals
asscssed for such billcts, Owing to lack of more complete
1nformation to scrve as a basis for weighting the items cited by
sCAp in the tr. 1 cost of living of occupation personnel, it is
not belie : that conclusive results as to comparative living
costs in Japan and the Unitel Statcs can be derived from these
data. In addition, consumer coods prices in the United States
have risen by some 20% durin the past year, occupation personnel
in Japan are entitled to as muoh as 30% exemption rrom Japanese
manufactures excice tax on purchases of souvenir items, and the
15 to 1 rate established in August l945 probably represented
come undervaluation of the yen at that time, It 1s felt probablo 
that these latter considerations were not fully coneidered in t
SCAP analysis,

.32

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.97)
3. It may be assumed that American ocoupation pers
in Japan and Korea should not be foroed to inour a redudej
standard of living because of service there though it need
follow that the prices of all artioles they purohase will be
the same relative levels as in the United States or in other
areas. Such factors as free transportation, very low prioee
for meals and personal services provided by the Army, moderateiy
priced billets, and supplies imported from the United States
and purchn : through Post Exchanges and Commissaries comprise
the major portion of the costs of living of occupation personnel
and their dependents and accordingly serve to offset the effeot
of hiher prices for irdigenous items purchased from the local
econony.
4. It is recognized that missionaries and others living ofr

and wio do rot have access to Army Post Exchanges and Commissar
iec, ad occupation personnel with families who are required to

and that the encouragement of religious and educational activity
in Japan is : primary objective of the occupation. As for the
effects ci a change in the military conversion rate to the
extent proposed by the Supreme Communder, it 1s felt that the
evaluation of the situatior provided by SCAP underestimates

a. SCAP is instructed in the basio directive (SWNCO

conversion of February 1946 and of the extraordinary tax
program. The latter is about to be implemented in Japen

and a change in the dollar yen military conversion r
on the soale recommended by SCAP, despite the limited

controls, limited to a cash income of 50o yen per month,
from his wages and about 2o0 yen from his saving. While an
influx of new currency into the Japanese economy emounting
to only 2% of total sales may not have any drastic overall
inflationary effect, amounting in the course of a year to
about 5% of the present currency circulation in Japan, the
fact nat occupation expenditures are made principally in
certain areas where personnel are concentrated may well have
a substantial effect on prices in those areas, particularly
for items on which expenditures are concentrated.
b. While the military conversion rate is not a
commercial foreign exchange rate, it has been used as the
basis for outpayment against dollar remittances, payment
against foreign curroncy receipts for repatriates, and may
be regarded by the Japanese as providing some indication of
the future foreign exchange value of the yen. If the 75
to 1 rate were adopted, the possibility exists that the
new rate might acquire quasi-official status by those
planning investments in export industries in Japan as well ae
those engaging in industries dependent upon imported goods.
In case  is subsequently decided that a substantially
high. value for the yen is desirable from the standpoint of
foreign competitors with Japanese exports and in the light
of Japan's need for imports, the necessary adjustments
within Japan would have some disruptive effects. SCAP
officials have stated that no basis exists at present for
predicting the future foreign exchange value of the yen.

by SCAP, attributable to an acute shortage of goods of the

in his analysis, SCAP indioates that the supply of the
gocds purchased by the American personnel is quite inade-

Yen to Dollar
A preliminary examination of available indexes of prices
and wages in Japan as compared with similar indexes for the
United States shows a wide divergence of possible exchange parit-
1es between the yen and the dollar. Current parities as derived
from m st of the available indexes, however, would be located
somewhere between 25 and 50 yen per dollar. A notable exception
to the 50 yen upper limit is found when full allowance is made
for black-market prices in the cost of living (consumers' price)
index. The results derived by the use of different price series
are shown below. Since the choice of a base period for computing
parities is c tremely important, more than one base year is used
for each iex where data are available. It should be noted that
caution in interpreting the data is necessary because of the
limitations of Japanese index numbers as well as the uncertainty
and instability of the current price situation in Japan.
Comparative Prices of Japanese Export-Import Commodities
In the absence of a commercial rate of exchange between
Japanese yen and Us dollars, comparisons of yen prices as regist-
ered by the Japanese Board of Trade with dollar prices for some
commodities as reported by officials of the US Commercial Cor-
poration can be used to indicate price relationships. Besiues
the instability of international prices for even a single com-
modity, here are cther shortcomings in this comparison, e.B.,
the fact that these prices do not necessarily reflect the prise
that vould be reached were commodities traded privately. In
addition, & ubstantial part of the items traded represent
withdrawals from stocks and may not reflect current costs.
The most important Japanese export item at present is raw
silk, which accounted for about one-half of the yen value of
exports for the past year. The price of raw silk in Japan 1s
set at approximately 12o yen per pound. The US Commeroial Cor

The index of daily wages in manufaoturing industrid

calculate the rise in daily wages in Japan. The Japanese ind

by the corresponding changes in the US Department of Labor,
Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) index for average hourly wages
in manufacturing industries in the United States. The resultant

average rate of exchange in the base period. The following

Parity Computed from Retain Price Indexes
The great range of parities derived from the use of retail
prices indexes is caused by black-market expenditures as well
as the shifting patterns of consumer expenditures in postwar
Japan. Because of these difficulties, two indexes are used:
an index of retail prices compiled by the Bank of Japan which
reflects official prices, and a consumers' price index recently
compiled by the Cabinet Bureau of Statistics which is heavily
weighted by black-market prices and consumer expenditures for
food. It shculd be noted that some economists argue against
relying heavi". on black-market prices in calculating purchasing
power parities, since these prices reflect not only relative
scarcities but also risk of detection and punishment for illegal
dealings. Computations based on both of these indexes have been
made for several base years and have been adjusted by changes
shown in the b prices as reflected in the Us Department of Labor
FLS Consumers: rice Index. A tabular summary of the derived

Limitntions of Parity Theory
There are several limitations to the general application
of purchasing power parity as applied to Japan. At the risk of
(versimplification some of the pertinent facts are indicated
below. The weakness of Japanese index numbers as measures of
relative charge and the instability of prices under present
inflctionary conditions in Japan have been previously mentioned.
The base years from which projections are made require further
study since the currency in the base years may have been cver
or undervalued. In addition, there are arguments against the
applicability of purchasing power parity in a country where
there is price fixing, rationing, allocation of materials, etc.
For example, if the price index does not change for goods thet
are rationed or almost entirely unavailable in one country and
this index is compared to a country not under such controls,
the application of purchasing power parity would appear to be
of limited use.
A number of factors enter into a determination of Japan's
rate of exchange quite apart from relative prices or costs of
production. For example, some overvaluation of the yen in terme
of dcllars may be desirable to place Japan in a position vhere
it is able to pay for necessary imports without relying upon
foreign credits. This statement is based on the assumption that
SWNCC 543/1

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.97)
8 E C R E T
Japan will not be able to increase exports substantially 
successive devaluations of the yen since its ability to e
production will probably continue to be severely limited for
next year or two. Furthermore, it may be assumed that emand
for Japanese export goods will be sufficiently great to keep
export prices at some high level during this time. Overvaluation
vis-a-vis US currency would also be in line with the policy
pursued enerally by most European countries. In addition,
certain important trading countries may object to potential
encroachment of Japan on the world trade scene through the
device of a low-valued yen. The danger that an overvaluation
of the yen y stimulate unessential imports does not seem
reat in a country in which large sectors of foreign trade will
be under government control at least for the next year or two.

APPENDIX "C"
DRAFT CABLE TO SCAP
Reurlet AG 123.7, 7 Nov. 1946-FD, subject "Revision of Yen
Dollar Conversion Rate."
Part 1. State, War and Navy Depts. have agreed and you are
hereby authorized to change the military conversion rate from 15
yen equals 1 dollar, to 50 yen cquals 1 dollar, for both Japanese
and Korean yen. Effective date for new rate will be established
by you and should be reported prior to implementation. New rai
of 50 yen equal: i dollar will be used on or after effective date
for conversion of military payment certificates, dollar currency,
dollar instruments and dollar remittances into Japancse and
Korean yen; and any other appropriate purposes for which the 15
to 1 military conversion rate is presently employed.
Part 2. Change in military conversion rate to full extent re-
quested by you might, it is considered here, have some results
contrary to your occupation objective of controlling inflationary
trends in Japan and Korea, and further that a reasonable margin
should be preserved so that the military conversion rate will not
place a lower value on the yen in terms of dollars than will be
cstablished by the commercial rate of exchange when ultimately
set. It is believed also that the objectives cited by you, can
in part be achieved by other means as indicated Part 3 this radis.
Part 3. Fr: the purpose of making available to members of the
occupation forces at reasonable prices, such quantities of
indigenous items as may be required to meet their reasonable needs,
you are authorized within your discretion to institute a program
as follows:
The Japanese Govt. should be directed to require Japanese pro-
ducers of indigenous items which can be purchased by the Central
Purchasing Office for sale to occupation personnel, to furnish
such items at yen prices, which when converted into dollar type

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SEORET
currency, will enable them to be sold by the Px's at prioes
are reasonably in line with similar conmodities in the United
States. Any difference between cost of production plus a reason
able profit to such producers, and the price at which the goods
are sold to the Central Purchasing Office, should be paid by the
Japanese Govt. as an occupation cost. As a supplementary measure,
Japanese producers shoulc be required to devcte such portions of
thcir productic.. capacities to supplying the Central Purchasing
Office as may be necessary to meet the reasonable requirements of
the occupation forces, insofar as this can be accomplished without
depriving the Japanese economy of goods essential to its minimum
pcaceful needs.
Part 4. The possibility of subsidizing services has been
oxamined; however, based on available information here as well as
information proviced to date by SCAP it is believed that the 50 to
1 rate will enable occupation personnel and their dependents to
procure adequate services at reasonable costs to meet their needs.
Part 5. Requcst you coordinate fully with other Allied
military forces to whom new rate will be applicable.
Part 6. Rate for Korea is an interim rate subject to further

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
PARIS 18th my 1947.
Mr. Minister,
When the war broke out between Japan and the
United States of America and England, I was Rumanian
Minister in Tokyo.
At the beginning of 1942, I exposed in a public
conference the theory against aggressiveness and published
during the same year LA NOUVELLE RELIGION which preached
harmony, creation, and condemned aggressiveness, and as
a consequence I was the object of two attacks on my life
(I add "La nouvelle Religion, 2 vols).
I was interned by the Japanese at Peking for three
years and was only released at the liberation.
The money I had in Tokyo (47,500 Swiss francs) was
confiscated also "more nipponico", that is to say. that I
was given by the Yokohama Specie Bank" of Tokyo, a letter
of credit in Yens ( 1 Sw. fr.= O.98 yen) on 27th October
1943, but as soon as I was away from the Diplomatic Corps
in Tokyo (which could have protested against this injustice)
the Japanese refused to pay me my money. The letter of
credit is now in Tokyo in the former Swedish Legation waich
represented Rumanian interests in Japan, and for which I
wrote to the American Authorities in Japan.
But in case this letter of credit cannot be found,
there ught to be a copy of same in the "Yokohama specie
Bank" which issued it on 27th October 1943 or around that
date.
Because I had raised my voice against the aggressors,
because I had published"LA NOUVELLE RELIGION" in 1942 in
Tokyo, therefore, in the very heart of the aggressiveness,
I left my career, my money was confiscated in Japan; and
the Rumanian Government at that time, by orders of the
Germans, stopped my rights immediately and confiscated all
my property.
The U.S.Authorities who found me in Peking, knoming
my sufferings and my activities, helped me to leave the

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
Far Rast and put seats in the plane and places in the steamer
at my disposal.
On arriving in New York I was interviewed by the Press
regarding Communism; I expressed my opinion and my beliefs very
freely. The interview appeared in the Wew York Times of
Saturday 3rd August 1946 under the title "Rumania described as
Anti-Communist". Owing to this fact, which was much commented
on, and to others similar, I was and still am unable to go back
to my country and am at present in Paris.
I am aduressing the U.S.Authorities begging them to
compel the "Yokohama Specie Bank" of Tokyo to reimburse me
the sum of 47.500 Swiss francs.
My money arrived in Japan in Swiss franes and was
transformed into Yens in accordance with Japanese aw.
In accordance with the same law, when a Diplomat leaves Japan,
the country must transform the Yens into Swiss franos, dollars
or pounds sterling.
I beg, therefore, that the Yokohama Specie Bank", Tokyo
be compelled to pay me the amount abusively confiscated in
1943 not in devalorised Yens but in Swiss Francs or U.S. Dollars
equivalent at the date of coufiscation (1 Swiss frane = O.98 Yen
or 3.75 Yen = 1 U.S.Dollar).
In the two volumes of LA NOUVELLE RELIGION annexed are
found several documents regarding which it is not the place to
insist on here.

Dear Mr. Harrison:
Your lotter of July 30, addressed to Mr Guthrie,
the Company's Washington representative, was referred to
me for actlon. I appreciate fully that radiotelegraph
service is important to the development of foreigm trade
in Korea and it is the desire of my Cempany te make its
service of maximum usefulness in this eonnectien.
Effective September 1, 1947, radiotelegreph serviee
will be extended to all United States occupied Kerea em
the basis suggested in your letter. Our representative im
Korea confirms that the Korean national telegraph system
is not suitable for handling this traffie, and our tariff
will specify that the Korean postal service will be used
for the collection and distribution of Radiograms. Only
dollars, or pre-paid vouchers will be accepted in payment
for our service until suitable arrangements are made for
converting Won into dollars.
The Company has accumulated in excess of ene million
Won from its operations in the Seoul area, and its Wen
holdings are increasing at the rate of more than 400,000
monthly The only me that can be made of this money under
present conditions is to pay rent, purchase sueh supplies
as are available on the local market, and pay salaries of
Korean personnel. These expenses total tmly about 70,000
Won per menth. Obviously, the Company cannot perpetuate
this situation, and beginning September 1, l947, our trans-
actions in the Seoul area, as well as those in other parts
of South Korea, will be limited to those for which we reseive
payment in dollars.. Pre-paid reply vouchers will, of course,
be accepted throughout Korea.

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
molosure to despatoh No. 99ll, November 20,
COPY
Paris, November 17,
Subjeot: Afternoon with General Bagulesco
In the course of an hour and a quarter conversation,
General Bagulesoo made it clear that he desires:
a) "High level intervention with the U.S. oooupation
authorities in Japan to secure restitution of approximately
47,000 Swiss francs which the Japanese took away from him
during the war;
b) assistance in seouring the transport to Paris of
considerable quantities of art treasures now stored in
Peking, for which the General has an authentioated list
attested by the U.S. Consul in Peking.
c) "in extremis", arrangements to permit him to go
to the United States.
The General is undoubtedly authentio and eocentrie.
As Rumanian Minister in Japan at the time of Pearl Harbor,
he published and ciroulated a book entitled "The New
Religion" (of which he presented me two copies) diotated
to the proposition that "the way of the aggressor is hard"
beeause he never wins, and that the world must unite to
preserve peace. For this indisoretion, he suffered twe
attempts at assassination and was interned by the Japanese
for the duration of the war. Liberated by viotorious U.S.
foroes, he was given every facility by them to go to the
United States, and possesses one dooument permitting him
to travel by a U.S. military aircraft. In the United
States he achieved some publicity by denouncing the present
Rumanian regime and its Soviet masters. In Paris, he has
given publio lectures to the same effect and has organized
publioly an association to propagate his ideas for world
peace and unity.
In connection with his request for dispatoh of his art
treasures, he intimated that he would be delighted to make
some available at reasonable prices to members of the U.S.
Embassy staff. Photographs and impressions whioh he showed
me were undoubtedly of very remarkable quality.
In connection with his desire to have his money restored.
he said that he had already written letters to our authorities
and that he had been informed by Mr. Patten of the Embassy
that the money could not be restored in Swiss franos, but pre-
sumably only in yen. He earnestly requested restoration in
some monetary unit that would be of value to him in Paris,
particularly because his Rumanian properties have already
sequestrated and sums which he has on deposit in a Brisimh
bank are completely blocked. In short, he is very hard u
I made no commitments naturally on any of the poimt
and went out of my way to explain the inevitable
involved in any such effort to reoover his funds 
I said I would bring his request to the attention
abassy authorities.

07D
1947, regarding the bank account of General Georges
Bagulesco, former Rumanian Minister to Japan, this
OCD
Headquarters initiated an investigation to verify the
allegations contained in General Bagulesco's letter
7C
of May 18, 1947, copy of which was forwarded with the
Department's reference instruction.
According to the Finance Division of the Economic
and Scientific Section of this Headquarters, the records
of the Yokohama Specie Bank, a closed institution in
process of liquidation, show an outstanding and partially
used letter of credit No. 5121, issued October 28, 1943,
in the original amount of ¥50,ó00 in favor of General
Bagulesco. The balance as of October 1, 1947, is
¥47,700. As stated in General Bagulesco's letter, the
letter of credit was purchased with Swiss francs at a
rate of 1 franc equals .98 yen. As all foreign exchange
transactions are prohibited by SCAP and as there is at
present no means by which anyone outside Japan can realise
Cat.
Lict.

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyo's 1424
Desember 1, 1947.
-2-
credit in non-Japanese currency for yen funds located
in Japan, it does not appear possible to effect
restitution of General Bagulesco's yen holdings in
Japan at this time.
The Economic and Scientific Section has further
advised that it is not considered that General
Bagulesco's funds are entitled to the guarantees pro-
vided in SCAPIN 1364 until more conclusive evidence
can be presented to substantiate his claim that he
was interned or that his property was taken, blocked,
or sold by the Japanese Government because he sym-
pathized with citizens, subjects, or countries at war
with Japan.
An independent investigation was undertaken by
United States Army intelligence authorities in Japan
to verify the allegations regarding his wartime sym-
pathies contained in General Bagulesco's letter under
reference. There is enclosed herewith a copy of a
1/
"Summary of Information" dated November 22, 1947, pre-
pared by these authorities following their investiga-
tion. It will be noted that there is no substantiating
evidence that General Bagulesco was sympathetic to
countries at war with Japan, that any attempts were made
on his life because of his alleged sympathies, or that
he was ever interned by the Japanese authorities in
Peiping. On the contrary it is reported that he was an
ardent supporter of the Tripartite Axis, that on several
occasions he denounced bitterly Great Britain and the
United States, and that he was provided by the Japanese
Government when he left Japan in October 1943 with
special facilities and shipping space for the transporta-
tion of 36 cases of his personal belongings.
Accordingly, this Mission does not consider that
sufficient evidence exists to extend to the funds of
General Bagulesco the guarantee provided by SCAPIN 1364,
November 27, 1946.
Respectfully yours,
brce.
W. J. Sebald
Acting Political Adviser

Enclosure to Despatch No. 1424, dated December 1

COPY
General Headquarters
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
General Activities
APO 500
22 November 1947
SUMMARY OF INFORMATION
SUBJECT: BAGULESCO, George
1. Following information has been obtained from
sources which are considered reliable by G-2:
a. Begulesco first arrived in Japan in February
1935 as Military Attache to the Rumanian Legation. Soon
after his arrival he showed great interest in the warrior
spirit of "old" Japan and became a student of the AkO
RoSHI (the story of the 47 Faithful Retainers or Ronins
famed in Japanese Kabuki plays and known as CHUSHINGURA).
He also became a member of the CHUO GISHI KAI (Central
Society to Perpetuate the Memory of the 47 Ronins). As
a result of his studies of the spirit of "old" Japan, he
published in February 1938 a book in French and English
entitled "Yamato Damashi" which is in essence a eulogy
of the Japanese warrior spirit.
b. In February 1939 Bagulesco returned to
Bucharest where he is said to have published 30,000
copies of the second volume of "Yamato Damashi.* It had
considerable success and was quickly sold out. In 1941
Bagulesco returned to Japan, this time as Rumanian Minister.
c. In 1942 Bagulesco, according to his own state-
ment, issued a publication in French entitled "La Nouvelle
Religion." Inasmuch as a copy of that book could hot be ob-
tained it is impossible to give it an evaluation. A
brief note on the book appeared in the Tokyo Asahi on
9 March 1943, stating that it dealt with the religious
aspects of the Japanese spirit.
d. The next book of Bagulesco, entitled "Rumania"
and published in 1943, clearly indicated that the author
was an ardent supporter of the Tripartite Axis and a
Rumanian jingoist who on several occasions denounced bit-
terly the democracies of Britain and the U.S.A. It was
further made evident that he fervently hoped the New Order
of Hitler would fully satisfy the Rumanian national and
territorial ambitions. (Translated excerpts of this book
were read by the investigator.)
. As Rumanian

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
•. As Rumanian Minister to Tokyo, Bagule
several public speeches in which he often expresse
anti-communist feelings. Nothing is kmown of his
having made public utterances in favor of the Alli
cause. On the contrary, Bagulesco, in his addressem
seemed to be firmly convinced that the Axis' New Order
was the right one and theirs would be the final viotory.
One source stated that he was always known as a Nasi
sympathizer.
f. No information is available on any alleged
attempt on the life of Bagulesco. It is known, however,
that he had some trouble with his driver which was not
of a political nature, but was caused rather by Bagulesco's
"unfairness" to his employees in money matters.
g. Bagulesco's relations with the Rumanian Military
Attache, General Radulesco, were reportedly none too
cordial. It is known that on 8 April 1943 Subject started
an argument with Radulesco because the latter supposedly
critieized the contents of Bagulesco's publication, "La
Nouvelle Religion." As a result of this argument Bagulesco
was dismissed from his diplomatic post on 10 April 1943,
and Radulesco succeeded him as Rumanian Minister to Tokyo.
h. Subject left Tokyo in October 1943 for Peiping,
China, accompanied by one Miss OTSUKA. Upon departure he
was provided by the Japanese authorities with special
facilities, and shipping space aboard the KOSHIN MARU to
transport 36 cases of personal belongings. No confirmation
is available of his own claim that he was subsequently in-
terned by the Japanese in China.
i. Investigation failed to disclose that Bagulesco
was interned at any time, or that his property was blocked
because he was sympathetic with the aims of the nations at
war with Japan.

ON EXCHANGE PROBLEMS
The Exchange Committee
Japanese Government
15 December 1947
NOTE:
The following is the sunmary of the recommendation on
exchange problems approved by the Exchange Committee on 12
December 1947 and subseguently approved by the Conference of
Economic Ministers on 15 December 1947, and is hereby presented
informally to the Yen Exchange Committee, ESS, as a reference
for its deliberation on the subject.
I. Ceneral Considerations
Measures concerning exchange rate or exchange factors should be
taken up as an integral part of the general internal and extornal
economic policies of the country; that is to say, merits or demerits
of a particular measure should be judged fron the standpoint of whether
it contributes to the currently primary task of achieving the earliest
stabilization of the economy.
During the past two years Japan has been slowly coning out of the
state of a closed economy. Any step we now take as regards exchange
problems will be necessarily in the direction of bringing the economy
into still closer relation with the international economic factors,
such as, external price structure. It is highly desirable that such
a step be made into an ocoasion for lessening the degree of the hot-
house character of the present day Japanese economy; but at the same
time, it should be enphasized tlat it should also be an occasion for
improving various external economic factors surrounding Japan. There-
fore, the Couaittee wishes to take this opportunity to lay special
stress upon the importance of necessary measuroe of assistance that
are complementary once a positive step is taken as regards exchange
problems.
II. Next Steps to be Taken
1. General Rate
(1) To be calculated as soon as possible. The matter of
making the rate public is to be decided with reference
to other aspects of the exchange problems.
(2)
The method of calculation is to be as follows:
a) The initial general rate is to be determined by
using the index of official prices (for Japan)
with a certain practical modification.
b)
Subsequent revisions are to be made in accordance
with a definite formula that is beyond political
control. The rate once established, however, is
to be maintained as long as possible.

=== Page 201 === (confidence 0.96)
Encl. No. 1 to
Tokyo's 1473,
December 23, 1947
-2-
2. The "Balancing Formula"
Some of the comaodities may be transacted at rates other
than the general rate; and there can be four such catogories,
namely: (designating the general rate by R)
in ¥ (in $)
a) Exports transacted at rates higher
than R.....

b) Fxports transacted at rates lower
x2 (x2)
than R.

Imports transacted at rates higher
than R.

d)
Imports transacted et rates lower
2 (m2)
Note:
Here, "rates higher than the general rate" means rates
at which yen is cheaper than at the general rate; and "rates
lower than the general rate" means the reverse.
The "balancing fonaula" may be written as follows:
 + M2 - (x1 + x2)
P.

 is recommended that the "balancing formula" is not to be
anplied very strictly at the be inning.
3. Invisibies (including cepital movements)
(1) As a rule, the general rate is to be appliod.
(2) Implemantation is to begin as soon as rossible.
4。
Coumodit" Umports
(1) As a rule, the general rate is to be applied. However, at
the outset it is to be applied, for most of the commodities,
only for accounting purposes.
(2) Implementation is to begin as soon as possible.
5。
Commodity Exports
(1) A multiple exchange factor system is to be adopted as an
interim measure with the rates differing from the general
rate to be established with the approval of the Yen Com-
mittee referred to below. The Committee, in approving the
rates, shall take into account as far as practicable the
"balancing formula". Exchange factors are to apply by
commodity groups.

allowed after strict examination.
(3) Implementation is to begin as soon as possible.
6. Yen Committee (tentatively so-called)
The "Yen Committee" is to be set up in the Japanese Govern-
ment; but details are to be worked out separately as soon as
possible.
7. Exchange Control
Legal steps are to be taken in order to nake the exchange
control effective as regards the multiple exchange factor system.

Subject: Views of the Japanese Government Concerning Exchange Problems.
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
1/
I have the honor to enclose copy of a summary of the recommenda-
tions on exchange problems prepared by the Exchange Committee of the
Japanese Government and approved by the Conference of Economic Ministers
on December 15, 1947, and subsequently submitted to the Foreign Bxchange
Rate Committee of the Economic and Scientific Section of this Head-
2/
quarters. A list of the members of the Exchange Committee of the Japan-
ese Government, a committee of experts, the Chairman of which is TSURU
Shigeto, Vice Chairman of the Coordinating Committee of the Economic
Stabilization Board, is also enclosed. The Conference of Economio
inisters is a conference of ministers of cabinet rank holding economic
KD
pbrtfolios and is comprised of the following members: WADA Hiroo,
Director General of the Economic Stabilization Board; KURUSU Takeo,
Inister of Finance; MIZUTANI Chozaburo, Minister of Commerce and
Thdustry; NATANO Kanae, Minister of Agriculture and Forestry; KITAMURA
Tokutaro, inister of Transportation; !TkI Takeo, Minister of Communi-
cations; and YONEkUBO Mitsusuke, Minister of Labor.
The position is taken by the Japanese Government that a general
AS
rate of exchange, to be determined by reference to price indexes, should
be established as soon as possible. Further, that where commodity trans
actions cannot be carried on at the generel rate, a special rate, either
above or below the general rate, will be established foraoh such
specific commodity. For the most part, it is anticipated th  commodity
imports, invisible,exports, capital imports and other capta transfers
will be conducted at the general rate. Cn the other hand a ultiple
exchange rate or factor system would be established for modity exports
with the approval of a Yen Committee to be set up by the Japanese Gov-
ernment.
It is believed that the decision by the Japanese Government to
establish a multiple system of exchange rates or factors for commodity
exports rather than a single rate has been predicated in large measure
upon the recognition of, and belief in the probable continued existence
of, the present wide disparity in the ratios of dollar and yen prices
of the various goods represented in Japanese foreign trade, by commodity
groups as well as by individual items within these groups, as indicated
in
Dist

=== Page 206 === (confidence 0.99)
Tokyots 1473,
December 23, 1947
-2 -
in the tables transmitted in this Mission's despatch No. 1437 of
December 6, 1947 on the subject of Transmission of Tables Concern-
ing Yen/Dollar Relationship Represented in Japan's Foreign Trade.
As was pointed out in that despatch, dollar prices placed upon
exportable items are not controlled by the yen cost of those items.
Furthermore, exports from Japan have been sold on a competitive
basis in most instances, whereas imports into Japan have been set at
an artificially low yen price in order to support price stabilization
policies of the Japanese Government.
The Japanese Government has apparently decided, in view of the
above, that the establishment of a single yen-dollar rate would not
only compel sellers of those commodities in which prices are currently
at a yen-dollar ratio above this rate to reduce the yen priee and pre-
sumably sell at a loss or stop production, but also would enable
sellers of commodities at a ratio below the general rate to raise their
yen prices and contribute to the inflation, or permit purchasers to
buy for less and thereby realize a twindfall profit" at the expense
of the Japanese foreign currency position. It is understood that con
sideration was given by Japanese Government officials concerned to the
desirability of establishing a single rate of exchange and utilising
a governmental subsidization program (within the scope of the subsidy
provisions embodied in the draft Charter for an International Trade
Organization) to meet the needs of those industries whose costs of
production would not permit them to continue at the established rate,
as well as the imposition of a type of export fee to reduce large
profits derived by those industries whose costs of production would
permit substantial price increases. However, the belief was expressed
that a system of multiple exchange rates would provide a more suitable
frame of reference for the conduct of private trade than a single rate,
and that a subsidization program on the other hand would present oppor
tunities for corruption of officials and businessmen. In this connection,
it is to be noted that no consideration has apparently been given in the
enclosed statement to the problem of administration of a multiple system
of exchange rates, which would involve, it is believed, in addition to
the problem of rate determination, continual pressures to raise indi-
vidual commodity rates as well as manifold opportunities for corruption
of officials and businessmen.
Although the general rate, once established, is to be maintained
at that level as long as possible, the plan as outlined envisages a
periodic revision of the general rate in accordance with a "balancing
formula" (as set forth in Enclosure No. 1), which is intended eventually
to effect a balance between the general rate and the ratio of all yen-
dollar ratios of commodity transactions carried on above and below the
general rate in Japanese foreign trade transactions. In this connection,
it is to be noted that unanimity of opinion does not exist within the
Exchange Committee of the Japanese Government concerning the mathematical
accuracy of the "balancing formula". Apart from the question of mathe-
matical accuracy, however, the Japanese Government officials concerned
anticipate that through periodic revision of the general rate in the
light of some "balancing formula" and through realistic adjustment of
the rates above and below the general rate, a balance will be reached
resulting in the use of a single factor or rate for all types of trans
actions.
Information

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.97)
Tokye's 1473,
December 23, 1947.
-3-
Information available to this Mission through contacts estab-
lished with Japanese officials concerned would indicate thet the
views expressed by the Japanese Government in regard to exehange
matters conform for the most part to the tentative views expressed
thus far by the Foreign Exchange Rate Committee of this Headquarters.
Although the enclosed statement does not treat the problem of
currency conversion after the establishment of a rate or rates of
exchange, it is assumed that at least for the near future the Japan
ese would not be permitted to receive foreign currency and non-Japanese
would be permitted to convert only limited quantities of foreim
currency for yen.
Respectfully yours,
W.Beane

Reference DEPTEL 6, January 8
Informal reply has aiready been made to General Shang
that SCAP does not favor creation of committee of
experts under furisdiction Allied Council. In conse-
quence General Shang has dropped suggestion.
There are aiready a large number of experts working
on exchange problem; to add another committee which
would have no suthority to make decislons would
appear to be impractical, would result in duplication
and uudue interference with duly established administra-
tive processes of this headquarters, and above all
48
else, would establish precedent for direct interference
in SCAP administrative functions by future committees
acting under Allied Counc1l. Throughout history of
Council, chairman has consistently ruled against
Council taking upon itself administrative funetions.
These rulings were based upon concept Couneil was
established solely to consult with and advise Supreme
Commander and that it would create an anomalous
situation for a subordinate advisory body to assume
powers not 1mplicit in its terms of reference. (In
this connection, please see verbatim minutes of thirs
meeting, discussion beginning page 1; ninth meeting,
JAN 26 1948
page 11; twelfth meeting, page 7).
