=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.99)
UNCLASSIFIED
Enclosure to Despatch No. 289 dated February 5. 1948,
from American Embassy, London, England.
February 2, 1948
Source: Daily Telegraph
STERLING CAN DO MORE FOR JAPAN THAN THE DOLLAR
Malcolm Muggoridge
Whatever view may be taken of the political
consequences of the Allied occupation of Japan, no one
who is familiar with the facts will deny that econom-
ically the country is in a bad way and moving towards
an economic crisis.
Though the yen is officially pegged at 5o to the
American dollar or 2o0 to the pound sterling, its black
market value is around 300 to the Amerioan dollar.
Thus yen prices are for the most part quite fictitious,
and the Japanese Government's accounting bears little
relation to the country's financial position.
The yen, in fact, is hopelessly inflated, and
month by month the process of inflation is continuing
at an accelerating rate. There are now about 180,000,000,000
yen in circulation, compared with 20,000,000,000 before
the war, and though, on Gen. MacArthur's orders, on
paper the current Budget has been balanced, currency is
still being printad at the rate of about 10,000,000,000
yen monthly. The present difficulty is to find supplies
of suitable paper to keep the printing presses working.
FINDING A REMEDY
Various devices have been adopted to deal with
this situation. For instanoe, a capital levy which was
instituted realised some 40,000,000,000 yen, and again
and again the Japanese Government has been urged by the
Supreme Command Allied Powers (S.C.A.P.) to make drastic
reductions in public expenditure.
What can be done in this direction is strictly
limited. The greater part of Government spending is
on occupation costs and the payment of officials!
salaries, neither of which items is susceptible of
much reduction.
On the contrary, as the cost of living rises both
items tend inevitably to increase. Two and a half
million Japanese Government employees and 250,000
working directly for S.C.A.P. already have the greatest
difficulty in providing themselves with the minimum
necessities of life. It has been .estimated that they
are foroed to spend 80 per cent of their earnings on
food alone.
If such/

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.99)
Page 2.
UNCLASSIFIED
Encl. to Despatch 289, February 2, 1948, from London
If such a state of affairs, unhappily only too
characteristic at this time, has not produced as
deplorable results in Japan as in the occupied terri-
tories in Europe it is because, thanks to Gen. MaoArthur's
firm handling of the situation, eoonomic difficulties
have not been intensified by political unrest.
The Russians, who in Europe have laboured with
considerable success to perpetuate a condition of poli-
tical and economic instability, have had in Japan little
opportunity to play a similar role.
SHRUNKEN OUTPUT
Another stabilising factor has been the fact that,
in consequence of the manner of the Allied occupation
of Japan, food production was not seriously interfered
with, so that relatively small supplies from the United
States ensured that there would be no large-scale
starvation.
Even so, the S.C.A.P. officials concerned, no less
than business men and bankers interested in Japanese
economic recovery, recognise that the present infla-
tionary situation cannot continue indefinitely. What
is required to check it in Japan as elsewhere is in-
creased production. At present production stands at
only 40 per cent of l934, and since the population has
inoreased in the interval by probably at least 15 per
cent output per head is equivalent to only about 35
per cent of pre-war.
Japanese economy before the war was based on
imported raw materials which are only scantily and in
poor quality available at home, and on the export of
cheap textiles and manufaotured goods, mostly to India,
China and other Asiatic markets.
The only substantial export trade with the United
States was in silk, which has now largely been eliminated
by the development of rayon.
TIED TO THE POUND
An attempt has been made since the war to work
up the export of American cotton to Japan. The
Commodity Credits Corporation provided $2lo,O00,000
(£52,000,000) for the purchase of surplus cotton in
Georgia and other cotton-producing areas in the United
States for export to Japan, there to be manufactured
into textiles for re-export.
This transaction has not been successful. The
staples sent were not suitable for Japanese looms, and
it proved even in the present time of scarcity ex-
ceedingly difficult to find a dollar market for the
textiles produced. Unless the exported textiles realised
dollars/

=== Page 220 === (confidence 0.99)
UNCLASSIFIED
Page 3.
Enol. to Despatoh 289, February 5, 1948, from London
dollars their sale did not cover the Commodity Credits
Corporation's original outlay.
Attempts to make a similar arrangement for the
import of wool from Australia have so far had little
success.
No less than 50 per cent of Japan's pre-war trade
was in sterling. The result was that, to all intents
and purposes Japan was economically and financially a
sterling area whose currency was in fact, if not in
theory, tied to the pound.
After the war the Americans, not unnaturally,
wanted to make Japan a dollar area, more especially as
they were forced to go to Congress for an appropriation
to finance the occupation. Indeed, they are still
putting dollars into Japan to the extent of at least
400,000,000(£100,000,000)a year.
BARTER TRADE
The only way to reduce and at last eliminate this
distasteful expenditure is to rehabilitate the Japanese
economy, and the Americans have now come to realise
that such rehabilitation is only possible in terms of
sterling trade.
The bookkeeping may continue to be in dollars,
but Japan can only thrive economically in so far as
she can import raw materials from sterling areas, such
as rubber from Malaya, cotton from India and the Sudan,
and wool from Australia, and export the goods manu-
factured from these raw materials likewise to sterling
aress
Various arrangements have been proposed, both
long-term and short, to facilitate such sterling trade.
They all amount essentially to a system of barter
whereby sterling imports are made to balance sterling
exports over a period of time.
A recent agreement announced in Tokyo but not yet
finally approved contained a provision that if at the
end of six months any balance remained, whether debit
or credit, it should be convertible into dollars. It
was this provision which led the British Government to
hesitate. In present circumstances not even a hypothetical
dollar commitment can be undertaken.
Even so, some sterling trade is being conducted,
notably with Hongkong, where the soundest currency in
the Far East and freedom from the controls which strangle
other ports make trading convenient and profitable.
A more ambitious arrangement now under considera-
tion in Washington and London would involve the use of
Japan's/

=== Page 221 === (confidence 1.00)
Page 4.
UNCLASSIFIED
Encl. to Despatch 289, February 5, 1948, from London
Japan's gold reserve, amounting to about $135,000,000
(£33,750,000), as security for dollar and sterling
trading credits to be furnished jointly by British and
American banks. An objection to this proposal is that
until the question of Japanese reparations is settled
the gold reserve cannot properly be considered as
authentic collateral.
Indeed, uncertainty about reparations is altogether
an impediment to Japanese economic recovery. At Kure,
where Japan's vast war industry plant stands silent
and devastated, near the largest naval docks ever con-
structed by man, I saw much salvaged machinery neatly
arranged and labelled ready for distribution as repara-
tions, but so far none has been distributed.
AMERICA'S LEAD
The devslopment of two-way trade between Japan and
countries within the sterling area is recognised by all
parties concerned as desirable, though naturally the
British Government will not unduly exert itseIf to pro-
mote Japanese exports liable to compete dangerously
with British products.
It is simply a matter of finding a suitable
arrangement for carrying on such trade. If so far a
suitable arrangement has not been found it is more in
consequence of the complexities of the post-war situ-
ation and of the cumbersomeness of triangular dis-
cussions between Tokyo, London and Washington than of
any deliberate holding back on the British or American
side.
S.C.A.P. officials concerned with Japanese trade
matters were particularly insistent on the desirability
of there being a British Treasury representative in
Tokyo with whom it would be possible to treat direotly.
As things are now, every agreement tentatively reaohed
in Tokyo has to be submitted for approval to Washington
and London--a lengthy and unsatisfactory procedure.
The British Government has been unable for obvious
reasons to frame or put into effect a positive policy
in the Far East. In the circumstances, as Mr. Gascoigne,
the head of the British Mission in Tokyo, is the first
to admit, Great Britain's role in Japan must inevitably
be at this time loyally to accept American leadership
in the post-war period as during the Pacific campaign
itself, while tactfully but firmly safeguarding essen-
tial British interests.
BRITISH PRESTIGE
Happily, these essential interests, political as
well as economic, have been safeguarded. Under the
direction/

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.97)
Pago5.
UNCLASSIFIED
Encl. to Despatch 289, February 5, 1948, from London
direction of Gen. Robertson and his officera the British
part in the occupation has been carried out in a manner
calculated to enhance the prestige of the British
Commonwealth, while Japanese economic recovery cannot
but lead to--indeed, depends on--the development of
mutually advantageous trade between Japan and the ster-
ling area.
If this economic recovery was important before,
it has been made a hundred times more important by the
rapid disintegration of China and the increasingly
aggressive tactics of Russia in Asia as in Europe.

sUBJECT: Cable to SCAP Rogarding Commercial Exchange Rate.
1. It is understood that the subject of a commercial rate or
rates of exchange was discussed by Mr. Green with SCAP personnel
and, although the timing and type of rate seems open to debate,
Files
Centrel
00/894.5151/3
the desirability of making progress towards such a rate seems to
be generally agreed. The same impr@ssion has been obtained by OE
from Dr. Fine and other SCAP personnel who have recently visited
Washington. Since the Kennan mission has now returned,the attached
cable is resubmitted to NA for clearance.
2. Discussions at the FEC on the exchange rate question were
initiated prior to the planning for the Kennan mission. The Nether-
lands, U.K., Australia and other countries, have evidenced consider-
able interest in this subject. No U. S. position has been taken in
the Commission on this subject, nor is it considered proper that
the Commission issue a policy statement regarding it. The "privat
trade" paper that has been in FEC, Committee No. 2, for several
months provides for the establishment of an exchange rate as soon
as practicable.
3. The establishment of a commercial exchange rate is considered
a somewhat unique and highly technical problem, especially when re-
lated to matters such as foreign investment, exchange control, import-
CSBM
export licensing, etc. Recent visitors from the Foreign Trade and
other divisions of SCAP's Headquarters have all stated informally
that SCAP would welcome a small group to assist in working out these
problems, and coordinating decisions with agencies here. Consider-
ing the length of time that was required to get agreement on the
change in the military conversion rates for Japan and Korea (about
six and eight months respectively) when negotiations were by eable
results of the mission that was sent out last summer to arran  the
reopening of Japan to limite i private trade is a precedent. bher
considerations that have prompted OE to recommend the sending a
mission are:
NTIAL FILE
(a) SCAP personnel have indicated that June 1948 has
894515
been favorably discussed as a desirable time for setting
the rate.
(b) SCAP
2948

=== Page 230 === (confidence 0.98)
CONPIPENTIAL
- 2 -
(b) SCAP people have indicated they desire a multiple
rate system. The U. S. has opposed such systems for other
countries, and the U.K. and other FEC countries have expressed
opposition. To effect agreement on this point will certainly
involve lengthy on-the-spot discussions. Unless all inter-
ested Washington agencies are represented in such discussions,
final decisions will be long delayed.
(c) The National Advisory Council has a special and
perhaps pre-eminent interest in this subject. Mr. Southard,
of the Treasury, has indicated that the Council would probably
be willing to delegate considerable responsibility to a
mission of the type proposed, having first reviewed and
approved the general principles under which the rate would be
established and operated. A SANACC paper incorporating these
principles has been prepared and will shortly reach NA for
clearance.
(d) A determination as crucial as a commercial exchange
rate must of course be based on the latest and most dofined
data available. This is particuarly true of such a dynamic
economy as Japan at the present time. Information thus far
or likely to be obtained here in Washington does not conform
to this requirement.

We strongly favor, of course, any steps which can
be taken to expedite the establishment of an exchange
rate. My conversations with Pickelle and Biplat of Ess
led me to believe that SCAP would welcome, and is ex-
pecting, the trade mission described in this telegram.
Certainly in theory, the presence in Tokyo of represen-
tatives of key Washington agencies vo uld not only ob-
viate time-consuming exchanges of cables between Tokyo
894.5151/4-148
and Washington but would strengthen the hand of those
divisions in ESs, such as Finance, Industry and Tex-
tiles, which favor the early establishment of a rate.
There are three conditions on which we should be
satisfied before endorsing the creation of this mission:
1. The mission should consist of those who favor
early action on establishing a rate.
2. The mission should receive broad authority from
the National Advisory Council so that it can act on
the spot. Otherwise, I envisage long delays while
Washington andTokyo debate the niceties of the problem.
3. The mission should be in Japan by June 1948
(which is considered a desirable time for setting the
rate), regardless of whether or not SANACC has approved
the exchange rate paper. The NAC will probably en-
dorse this paper within the next fortnight.
I have talked these points over at length with
McDiarmid, and I am convinced that OE is as interested
as we are in seeing that the "right" persons are se-
lected, that the mission has full authority, and that
it arrives in Japan by June, regardless of the stata)
of the SANAcC pap er. I, therefore, recommend clearepse
of both the telegram and the draft SANACC paper.

=== Page 235 === (confidence 0.98)
CONFIDENTIAL
DATE: Apr11 9, 1968
TO
FE - Mr. Hamilton
NA - Mr. Allis
FROM :
SANACC Paper on Establishment of Exchange Rate
SUBJECT:
1. The principal reason why we have not permitted
the exchange rate to be fixed by the Japanese is the
one you mention, namely, that it is too technical and
important a matter for the Japanese to decide. The
problem has been under exhaustive study by a special
SCAP Headquarters committee for the last four months,
and the committee's findings have recently been publishede
Central
It would occasion considerable delay if the problem wer
9
now turned over to the Japanese.
2. The advantages of a special mission are twofold:
e. It will speed up the determination of an
exchange rate to have direct consultations between Head-
quarters officials and the Japanese Government on the
one hand, and State, War and Treasury representatives
on the other, provided that the latter are empowered to
speak with some final authority for their respective
Departments. This procedure will obviate time-consuming
cable exchanges between Tokyo and Washington.
b. There is at present some disagreement in
Tokyo as to whether the exchange rate should be instituted
in June (a position taken by the Finance and Foreign
Trade Divisions of ESs) or at the end of the year (a
CSBM
position taken by Research and Statistics and the Price
Control Divisions of Ess). It is our desire, and I am
informed that it is the desire of those who are to com-
prise the proposed mission, that the rate be fixed as
soon as possible. The presence of this mission in
Tokyo should help to resolve this inner Headquarters
conflict in favor of the earlier date.
3. The IATB is the Inter Allied Trade Board.
Formed by FEC decision in October 24, 1946, the IATB'
894,51
the FEC on trade matters and that its policy decision
shall be subject to the approval of the FEC. A brief
description of IATB operations will be found on page 2
of the blue manual "Activities of the Far Eastern Com-
IAL
mission".
4. The
FILE
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 236 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
- 2 -
4. The terms of reference of the Far Eastern Com-
mission require that the FEC ensure that Japan carries
out the Terms of Surrender. The Terms of Surrender,
in turn, require that Japan accept the provisions of
the Potsdam Declaration. The final sentence of Article
11 of the Potsdam Declaration states: "Eventual Japanese
participation in world trade relations shall be per-
mitted". The basic post-surrender policy (adopted by
the FEC in June 1947) interprets that statement to mean
the right of Japan to re-engage in trade with other
countries, and includes this statement: "Control 1s
to be maintained over all imports and exports of goods
and foreign exchange and financial transactions.  The
FEC shall formulate the policies and principles govern-
ing exports and imports to Japan. The FEC will formu-
late the policies to be followed in the exercise of
these controls". Whereas the language of the Basic
Post Surrender Policy is somewhat ambiguous, it would
still be difficult to argue that the FEC is not em-
powered to formulate policies governing foreign exchange
and financial transactions.
We already have a paper (C2-273/3) in Committee 2
of the FEC entitled "Conduct of Private Trade with
Japan". The final paragraph of this paper states:
"A system of import and export licensing may be
employed to ensure compliance with foreign trade
policies and regulations formulated in accordance
with the terms of reference of the FEC. A com-
mercial exchange rate (or rates) for the yen
should be established as soon as practicable.
If C2-273/3 is in the FiC at the time we are about
to fix an exchange rate, it might be advisable for us
first to issue an interim directive merely stating that
a commercial rate of exchange should be established for
the yen. This, I should think, would relieve us of any
responsibility to submit the attached SANACC paper to
the FEC, for we may correctly claim that the interim
directive thus filed with the FEC has directed SCAP to
establish an exchange rate, whereas the SANACC paper
merely provides for the implementation of that policy.
It is highly important that this detailed SANACC
paper be not submitted to the FEC where interminable
delays would be experienced in its adoption. I believe
that the procedure recommended above will obviate those
delays without abandoning prescribed international
channels.

problems involved have been discussed with Mr. Draper's
group. Before giving FE's clearance, you may, therefore,
wish to have NA check the outcome of these discussions.

My dear Mr. Whitman:
Tie
I thank you for your letter of the 14th concerning
the 4ooo yen that were given to me as pay by the
2

148

vou ask for certain information concerning the basis
on which the Japanese paid me. My record shows that
the Japanese started paying us in August, 1942; that
they paid us on the basis of 170 yen per month, and
we were told that they were paying us the same amount
that they paid officers of the same grade in their
own army.
They first started out by giving me 19 yen 83 sen in
CS/A

The balance of my monthly pay was to be credited to
me and deposited by the Japanese authorities in the
Japanese postal savings system. The deductions men-

say about this arrangement.

to show the amount of balance due us. A statement of

It will be noted from the foregoing that at the time

made from month to month as our captors reported to
us our respective balances.
It is my contention that this is not a claim such as
would be covered by the bills introduced in the last
Congress, providing for the creation of a Claims
Commission to pass upon claims of former prisoners of
war against the Japanese government. It is my conten-
tion that the claim here involved is for funds which
the Japanese should have paid us according to the rate
of exchange existing at the time the payment was due.
I understand that early in l942 the Japanese officially
pegged the yen at 8 to 1. As I have the yen, I see no
reason why in fairness and justice they shouldn't be
required to redeem the yen at the figure mentioned.
Cur captors admitted the indebtedness to us and they
actually paid me in yen. At the time they made settle-
ment with me, however, the yen had depreciated to the
extent that it was practically worthless, whereas when
the Japanese withheld that money, which they admitted
owing me, and applied it to their own war effort, it
was reputed to have been worth from 5 to 8 yen to a
dollar. Therefore, I submit that the Japanese govern-
ment should be required to redeem the yen on the basis
of its value at the time they applied it to their war
effort, which was the effect of it's deposit in the
Japanese postal savings account.

EYES ONLY Telegram from General MacArthur
The meeting which I attended this morning at the Pentagon
at your request turned out to have been called by Major General
G. F. Richards (Army Comptroller) to consider the attached
telegram. Mr. Hilliard (O), Mr. McDiarmid (OE), Mr. Strong (FN)
and I were there from State, in addition to several Treasury
representatives. General Richards, who has been requested by
Mr. Draper to act for him in developing proposals on exchange
rate problems in Japan, stated that the telegram had been seen
only by Mr.Draper,Colonel Feyereisen and himself, and requested
that the utmost secrecy be preserved in regard to it.
N8
The telegram, which I suggest that you read in its en-
84
tirety, proposes the establishment of a new military exchange
rate of 225 yen to the dollar. You will see at the bottom of
page 5 of the telegram how this figure was arrived at. You
will also see, at the bottom of page 4, that General MacArthur
considers that "the requisite conditions precedent to the es-
tablishment of an international (commercial) rate are in a
process of slow evolution, while remedial action on the mili-
tary rate becomes daily more urgent". General MacArthur goes
on to say that setting of the higher military rate "will in
no way prejudice the establishment of the international rate
at the propitious time" and that "it should be understood, of
course, that foreign trade transactions will in no way be af-
fected by any change in the military rate".
General Richards and Colonel Feyereisen took the position
that the present military rate of 50 yen to the dollar was
working considerable hardship on our forces in Japan, and
that for this and other reasons mentioned in General MacArthur's
telegram an upward revision of the rate was desirable at this
time. They noted that both General MacArthur and the Draper
Mission believe that it will be some time before establishment
of a commercial exchange rate will be practicable, and that
4
in the meantime it is imperative that the military rate be
OO
fb
raised to a level approximating purchasing power parity.
5751
The State representatives generally took the position
S
that it would be practicable and desirable to set a commercial
exchange rate in the very near future, as soon as a State-Army-
CRET FILE
DC/R
4.2748
:..L
2
1956
TOP SECRET
Cat
FILED

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SECRET
- 2 -
Treasury mission had been able to go to Japan to conduct .the
necessary on-the-spot investigations. They maintained that
the reasons which have been advanced for postponing of the
setting of a commercial rate will be just as valid six months,
a year, or two years hence as they are today, and that the
best thing to do is to set a rate now with the understanding
that this rate is not fixed and permanent but can be altered
if developing circumstances indicate the need. Mr. McDiarmid
questioned whether the Draper Mission had had opportunity
really to investigate this question, which was outside, or
at least marginal, to its announced purposes in Japan, and
noted the differences which are known to exist within SCAP
Headquarters on the problem. Both he and the Treasury repre-
sentatives questioned General MacArthur's statement that the
establishment of a new military rate would in no way prejudice
the establishment of a commercial rate. They maintained that
a military rate which, for example, undervalued the yen, would
lead to a loss of confidence in the yen and would have an un-
avoidable effect on the determination of a commercial rate at
a later date.
The general position of the State and Treasury represen-
tatives, in short, was that the first step should be for an
inter-Departmental mission to go to Japan as soon as possible
to study the question of the establishment of a commercial
rate, and if it was decided that such a rate should be estab-
lished without further delay, to make recommendations as to
what the new commercial and military rates should be. If,
on the other hand, the mission concluded that it would be un-
desirable to establish a commercial rate until, say, the ond
of this year, we should then consider the question of whether
the military rate should be raised in the interim. It was
decided that the State and Treasury representatives should
consult with their superiors in their respective Departments
to determine whether this was the official State and Treasury
position, and then to meet again with General Richards to-
morrow, April 28, to consider the problem further.
at 3:00 P.m.
Mr. McDiarmid is obtaining the views of his office in
the matter, and Mr. Hilliard is obtaining Mr. Saltzman's
views. It is suggested that after FE has arrived at a ten-
tative position, today if possible, we coordinate with OFD
and O to present a single State position at the meeting to-
morrow.

Thia radio in 7 parts.
Fart 1. Since 12 March 1947 when the present mil-
itary conversion rate of 50 yen equals 1 dollar was estab-
lished the rate has progressively become more unrealistic as
a medium of exohange. At the time it was instituted it did
not approximate a fair valuation of the dollar in relation
to yen and the subseguent inflation of the yen has widened
the gap to such an extent that a revaluation is now essent-
ial in the interest of facilitating legitimate transactions.
The inequity of the present rate is now so pronounced as to
jeopardize the success of enterprises which contribute mater-
Lally to the objectives of the occupation. Although strict
controls have been established and enforced this condition
serves to stimulate black market and extra-legal transactions
in currency and barter.
Part 2. One of the purposes of the military con-
version is to enable occupational personnel to make legimate
and open market purchases of indigenous foods and services
at reasonable prices. It is not possible to reduce occup-
ational personnel and their dependents to an existence dep-
cndent solely on United States supplies and services. It
:s inevitable that they will purchase native items and
services. To the extent that such purchases are maãe the
 lo i ' acts in effect as a reduction in pay. This
ate is contributory to moral relaxation of United States
Irsonnel and therefore detrimental to the maintenance of a

cetera. In certain instances their dollar outlays
for these purposes along have exceeded their total
ccllar income for the same period.
C. Air-line operators. Of necessity they must
hire Japanese labor and either lease or establish
certain operating facilities. The expense incident
to these normal operations has developed into a real
handicap which hampers their further development.
D. Tourists. The Japanese government and priv-
ate business men, as well as United States airlines
and shipping companies, have been endeavoring to dev-
elop tourist traffic in Japan. With the existing
military conversion rate the cost of even minor
souvenir items, ordinary amenities, and expenses in-
cident to travel are prohibitive.  It constitutes
the greatest obstacle to the development of this pro-
ject.
E. Recipients of transmissions. As in the case
of missionaries, Japanese recipients of dollar trans-
missions, out-paid in yen at the official rate, are
denied a fair equivalent for the dollar value.

: 0 - Mr. Saltsman
DATE: May 5, 1948

FROM : O - C. C. Hilliard 
5-28-48
SUBJECT: Exchange Rate for Japanese Yen
P....
Shortly after Mr. Draper's return to Washington from the Far East
last month, a teleconferenca was held between the Department of the Army
and members of General MacArthur's staff in Tokyo on the subject of a
change in the present military rate of conversion (50 yen equals $1.00),
pending the setting of a commercial rate of exchange for the Japanese yen.
Pursuant to this telecon and to conversations in the theater during
Mr. Draper's recent mission, two cables regarding this subject have been
received from CINCFE Tokyo, namely, C-60098 (CM-IN 4570 of April 21) and
C-60362 (CM-IN 461 of May 3). Copies of these two cables are attached.
In confirmation of our conversation of yesterday afternoon, comments
are set forth below under three headings, which I believe represent es-
pecially important aspects of this problem.
1. Paramount responsibility for the establishment of U. S. policy
governing the setting of a commercial rate of exchange for the
Japanese yen, or for authorizing a change in the present dlitary
rate of conversion for the yen:
Since the matter of a rate of exchange or of a military rate of
conversion for the yen, or for other currencies involving U.S. re-
sponsibility, is a matter of highest U. S. financial policy, I submit
that the Department of State has paramount responsibility among U.S.
Departments and agencies in this matter. In fact, during the period
when a rate for the Japanese yen was under discussion in connection
with the formulation of JCS directives to General MacArthur, the War
Department took the position that only as a matter of last resort and
lacking action by the State and Treasury Departments, would the.War
Department establish an exchange rate or military rate of conversion
for the yen. (This was also the War Department position regarding a
rate for the German mark.) The initial military conversion rate for
the yen was set by Secretary Byrnes in coordination with Secretary
Vinson. When the initial military rate of conversion was changed
from 15 to 1 to 50 to 1, the decision was reached in a meeting at
which former Under Secretary of State Acheson presided. As you
know, the Treasury Department did not concur in the change just
mentioned.
2. The principle of whether there should be a change in the
military rate of conversion for the yen, or the establishment of
a commercial rate of exchange for the yen, at this time:
TOP SECRET

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
.TOP SECRET
- 2 -
The Treasury Department has consistently maintained the posi-
tion that no change should be made in a military rate of conversion,
but that a commercial rate of exchange should be set at the earliest
possible moment. The reasons supporting such position are, for ex-
ample, that changes in a military rate of conversion bring about only
very temporary relief for military personnel and, at the same time,
have seriously unfavorable effects, such as increasing lack of con-
fidence in the local currency, i.e., the yen in this instance. Not
only is the psychological effect bad, but black market operations
are stimulated and administratively cumbersome procedures delay the
development of normal commercial business. I fully share the Treasury
views just indicated.
The rate now proposed by CINCFE is no longer a strictly military
rate of conversion but would apply also to some financial transactions
of a commercial nature and not to others. This straddling rate would,
I believe, be bad news for the American taxpayer and would represent
a good formula for thorough confusion and dissatisfaction.
State Department, as well as Treasury Department, views now
favor prompt establishment of a commercial rate of exchange for the
yen and not a change in the military rate of conversion for the yen.
General MacArthur is recommending the latter and Under Secretary
Draper is supporting General MacArthur's position. In this connection,
it is not certain that General MacArthur knows that State and Treasury
thinking now favors the early establishment of a commercial rate of
exchange for the yen.
A military rate of conversion for the yen was expected to be
temporary. It was never envisaged at the time of entry of U.S. troops
in Japan that a lengthy period would elapse before the establishment
of a rate of exchange for the yen. If the position were taken that
all factors have to be just right before a rate of exchange is estab-
lished, the use of a military rate of conversion could continue in-
definitely. I am convinced that, although any rate of exchange now
set would be under inflationary pressure and would have to be changed
from time to time, as has been the case in Italy, for example, the time
has now come for the State Department, in coordination with the Treasury
Department, to exercise courage and a vigorous power of decision and
insist that a commercial rate of exchange be established at the earliest
possible time, say by July 1 of this year. There is no doubt that urgent
attention should be given to General MacArthur's need for relief from
the present unrealistic rate of conversion of 50 yen to the dollar.
However, as set forth herein, the setting of an appropriate commercial
rate of exchange could give such relief to best all around advantage.
The National Advisory Council is charged with statutory responsi-
bility in policy matters of this kind and,I understand from the Treasury
Department,is favorably disposed toward the earliest setting of a
commercial rate of exchange for the yen.
OF SECRET

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SECRET
-3-
3. The actual rate of exchange to be established for the Japanese
yen, or the rate to which the present military rate of exchange
would be changed:
In principle, I believe that it is wise to fix an initial com-
mercial rate of exchange at a low rather than high value for the yen.
A deep cut in value would allow for a considerable period of time dur-
ing which all concerned could have reasonable certainty that there
would be no change in the yen rate. The experience in Italy, for
example, would bear out this thesis. In addition, a favorable export
position for Japan is not now an international political problem.
General MacArthur has recommended a new military conversion rate
of 225 yen to the dollar. Personally, I would be guided by his recom-
mendation, subject to NAC review, as to actual figures, including any
recommendations which he might make relative to the figure at which a
commercial rate of exchange should be set. I believe that it would be
better for the latter to be set at, say, 250 yen to the dollar rather
than at 225 yen to the dollar, in line with the above mentioned princi-
ple. However, the economics experts will wish to study data on this
subject and, in fact, arrange for a small mission, with NAc blessing,
to spend a short time in the theater on this aspect of the problem.
I would be guided by the views of such a mission, coordinated with the
views of CINCFE, provided that the commercial rate of exchange to be
established would represent at that time an undervaluation rather than
an overvaluation of the yen.
Attachments - 2.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
May 18, 198
U - Mr.Lovett
Subject: Establishment of Rate of Exchange for Japanese Yen.
For your information, General MacArthur, in a Top Secret (Eyes
Only) cable of April 21, 1948 to Under Secretary Draper, has requested
as a matter of urgency that the present military rate of conversion of
50 yen equal one dollar be changed to 225 yen equal one dollar and that
application of the conversion rate be extended to include all non-trade
financial transactions of Japan. In the above mentioned cable and in
a supporting cable of May 3, General MacArthur has maintained that, under
the present military rate of conversion, occupational personnel are frus-
trated in making legitimate, open-market purchases of indigenous foods
心
and services at reasonable prices; also that occupational personnel and
their dependents cannot be reduced to an existence dependent solely on
U.S. supplies and services. In addition to occupational personnel,
C
General MacArthur mentioned the hardships now caused by the present
military rate of conversion to missionary groups, business service agen-
cies, tourists, and other recipients of remittances. General MacArthur
stated that his headquarters has hoped that a general exchange rate for
the yen would be evolved within a reasonable time. He expressed the
opinion, however, that conditions requisite to the establishment of a
general commercial rate for the yen do not yet exist.
Views of the State and Treasury Department, as expressed to the
Department of the Army, are that it is highly desirable to proceed now
with the establishment of e general commercial rate of exchange rather
than to approach this subject piecemeal. The latter approach would be
exemplified by a further change in the military rate of conversion and
the extension of such rate to cover all non-trade international financial
transactions of Japan. It is believed that, as a practical matter, con-
ditions may not, over a lengthy period, be more satisfactory than at
present for the establishment of a general commercial rate of exchange
for the yen. In this connection, representatives of other interested
countries, such as the British and the Dutch, have expressed the hope
that an exchange rate for the yen can be set at the earliest possible
date.
A mission, comprising representatives of the Federal Reserve Board
and the Departments of State, the Treasury, the Army, and Commerce, has
left by ATC for Japan on May 15 under invitational orders from the Depart-
ment of the Army to study the yen rate problem. Mr. O. J. McDiarmid, OE,
is the
CEdid 201RodSEXert

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.98)
TOP SECRET
-2-
is the State Department member of the mission, which is headed by
Mr. Ralph A. Young, Associate Director, Division of Research and
Statistics, Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System. This
mission is to consult with General MacArthur and his staff on the above
subject and return to Washington within approximately four weeks from
the date of departure of the mission. It has been agreed among the
State, Treasury, and the Army Departments that a U.S. Government posi-
tion on the establishment of a general rate of exchange for the yen
should be reached by July 1, 1948 and that, if such position is not
reached by that date, it would be necessary to proceed with a change in
the present military rate of conversion for the yen, as an interim
measure. The level at which a general rate of exchange or a new military
rate of conversion would be set is considered a matter for consultation
among interested U.S. Departments and Agencies, including the National
Advisory Council, and such level would depend largely on the findings
of the above-mentioned mission.
It is considered desirable that no publicity be given to the mission
nor to the above subject in general at this time. However, in order to
be prepared for possible questions at the Secretary's press conference,
the text of a proposed reply by the Secretary to such questions is now
in process of coordination by the Department with the Treasury and Army
Departments. As soon as the text, of a brief, general nature, has been
agreed upon among State, Treasury and Army, a memorandum will be sent
forward to S/s.
cs.
Charles E. Saltzman

D: M 6, 1948
O - C. C. Hilliard .
Central
SUBJECT:
Proposed Change in Conversion Rate for Japanese Yen
Document Must Be Returned To
Following are reasons for not changing the present military conver-
sion rate of 50 yen to the dollar prior to the early setting of a commer-
cial (international) rate of exchange for the yen, even if eventually,
due to unavoidable delay in setting an exchange rate, it may be found
necessary to take interim action in the matter of a change in the conver-
sion rate:
1. It is unsound, in principle, to approach General MacArthur's
problem from the standpoint of a change in the military conversion rate
or of a change to a conversion rate of broader scope than the present
military rate. This is because a conversion rate continues and aggra-
894.5151/5-648
vates present uncertainties and complicated procedures and is discrimi-
natory, whereas a commercial rate. would get at the roots of the problem
and would be a big step toward bringing the Japanese economy back inte
realistic relations with the economies of other countries.
2. It is recognised that CINCFE has a real and urgent problem, but
the setting of a commercial rate of exchange would also give CINCFE the
relief which he now seeks by means of a change in the military conversion
rate.
3. It should take no longer to reach agreement on setting a commer-
cial rate of exchange than on a change in the military conversion rate;
a commercial rate has to be set sooner or later, and State and Treasury
are now agreed that now is the time to act on a commercial rate.
4. The argument of CINCFE that conditions are not in shape for the
immediate setting of a commercial rate of exchange could no doubt be
advanced for months and years to come. Courage and vigorous decision
should be exercised to set a commercial rate now rather than later.
5. A change in the military conversion rate would increase a loss
of confidence in the yen with an accompanying increase in black market
operations, and would probably prejudice beyond repair the early setting
of an appropriate commercial rate for the yen.
In general, I should like to add that I see no good reason for
incantations and stage settings in connection with studies and a mission
to Japan on this subject. On the contrary, I am convinced that a small
governmental group, with NAc blessing, and a minimum of fanfare are
essential. Otherwise I foresee undue delays and the likelihood of 
Congressional and international political complications, which I subat
FILED
could and should be avoided to the maximum possible

DRAT
(For SANACC Minutes)
May 10, 1948
In connection with the subject of the relationship between the rates
of conversion for the Japanese yen and the Korean won, Mr. Draper referred
to consideration which is being given by the State, Treasury. and Army
Departments to an urgent request from General MacArthur for a change in the
present military rate of conversion for the Japanese yen and the broaden-
ing of such rate to include also non-trade international financial tranmo-
tions, pending the setting of a general commercial rate of exchange for the
Japanese yen. Mr. Draper added that General MacArthur feels it would be
premature to set a general commercial rate at this time. Mr. Draper stated
on may
that at a meeting today in his office, at which, in addition to himself,
there were present Assistant Secretary of State Saltsman, Mr. Southard of
the Treasury Department, and other representatives of State, Treasury and
Army, an agreement had been reached, substantially as set forth below. A
statement of this agreement which, in accordance with further discussion by
the Committee, has been cleared by Mr. Saltzman, Mr. Draper and Mr. Southard,
1s as follows:
(1) Immediate consideration will be given to the practicability of
ostablishing a general commercial exchange rate for the Japanese yen;
(2) A technical mission representing interested U. S. Government
agencies will be sent as soon as possible to Japan for the purpose of study-
ing this problem and preparing recommendations regarding the determination
of a general commercial rate of exchange or, - if action regarding the
establishment of such rate is found by the mission to be premature, - a new
TOP SECRET
TOPSECRT

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.97)
TOP SECRET
-2
conversion rato for military and financial purposes, as an interim measure.
The Army Department and the theater will facilitate the work of the mission
in every way possible; and.
(3) If the U. 8. Government does not reach a position by July 1. 1948.
on a general commercial rate, including such NAC action as the MAc may deem
necessary, the Department of the Army and CIncrr would be justified in going
ahead with a change in the military rate of conversion to be applicable to
military and non-trade international financial transactions. The level of
the new conversion rate would be set after consultation with the State and
Treasury Deparments.

Mr. Moseley, SANACC

DRAFT CABLE TO SCAP
Reurad C 61913, June 29, 1948:
Part 1
Regarding establishment single general exchange rate, considerations
advanced urad have been carefully examined by Army, State and Treasury
Departments, whose reaction is as follows:
1. Difficulties to be overcome in achievement stable Japanese economy
are fully appreciated by all as ington agencies concerned. Unanimous
opinion here, however, is that stabilization efforts must be greatly
intensified to be successful, and that provision rehabilitation GARIoA
and oJEIRF funds permitting institution definite rehabilitation program
makes this the appropriate time. Lor reasons advanced in Young Report
and reviewed in part below, it is considered establishment general rate
by October 1 will materially assist achievement economic stabilization and
recovery goals. It is believed that failure to establish general rate and
concomitantly to intensify stabilization measures would mean further
deterioration econo: ic situation to point where U.S. rehabilitation aid
might be largely wasted, internal adjustments necessary for efficient
utilization of proäuctive resources would be further postponed, and
possibility general monetary and fiscal collapse would have to be faced.
2. rolitical aspects as well as others rasied urad have been
carefully considered. Views here coincide fully with objectives outlined
para 4 urad relative to institution of cemocratic form of government,
opportunities
TOP SECRET
0-H59

TOP SECRET
-2-
opportunities for self-development by the Japanese consistent with the
ideals of free people, and the development of a strong sense of self-
responsibility and majority rule by the Japanese. iowever, in the
matter of the extent of U.S. policy guidance to the Japanese with respect
to econoic principles which must be followed to lay the foundation for
a lasting democratic form of government, we believe it would be inconsistent
with our objective or earliest possible attainnent by the Japanese of the
above mentioned objectives for the U.S. to contenance Japanese economic

judgement of this Government, is essential to the maintenance of a

Commercial Rate of Exchange for the Yen
894.5151/5-1148
I have been informed by Mr. Hilliard (O)
that it was decided by Mr. Saltzman, Mr. Draper and
Mr. Southard at a meeting yesterday evening to send
a mission to Japan, in accordance with the attached
revised memorandum of agreement, to study the ques-
tion of a commercial exchange rate for the yen. The
target date for the departure of the mission is this
Friday, May l4. It was agreed that Ralph A. Young
of the Federal Reserve would be the technical head
of the mission, assisted by Arthur Hersey of the
Federal Reserve, McDiarmid of State, Arthur Stuart
of Treasury and Michael Lee of Commerce. It has
not been decided who the Army representatives will
be. The head of the mission is to be either Allan
Sproul, Winfield Riefler, or John H. Williams, in
that order of preference. Mr. Draper continues to
desire the inclusion of a non-governmental member
and has invited a Mr. Loree down from New York to
discuss his joining the mission. Mr. Saltzman,
Mr. Wood and Mr. Southardare opposed to having an
outsider go along.

SUBJECT: Repert of Exchange Rate Mission.
894.5151/6-2148
You will recall that a Mission headed by Mr. Ralph A.
Young was sent te Japan about three weeks age to leek inte
the question or the establishment ef a commeroial exchange
rate. The Missien returned June 17 and submitted the
attached report. At the same time General MacArthur,
although complimenting the Mission on its diligent and
erudits approach to the problem, has sent in a personal
telegram (copy attached) sharply opposing the Missien's
essential recommendations. The views expressed in his
telegram are based on a lenger, technioal memorandum pre-
pared by the SCAp Foreign Exchange Board. A copy of this
memorandum is also attached.
The Missien's recommendations are well summarized on
pages l-5 ef its report. In briefest terms, the recom-
mendations are that a "controlled type, single general
rate" be established at the earliest possible date, net
later than Ootober 1, 1948; that the rate be within a
range of 270 yen to 330 yen per dollar (you may reoall
that SCap's proposed new military conversion rate was
225 yen per dollar); that 1f S0AP feels that immediate
action to raise the present "severely inequitable" mili-
tary conversion rate is necessary, he might raise it as
soon as July l, with the new rate set at any level up to
270 yen per dellar; that raising of the military conver-
sion rate should not be undertaken unless 1t has been
previeusly deoided that a general rate shall be establish-
ed in October; and that the period from now until the
setting of a general rate on Ootober lst sheuld be util-
ized to institute a series of measures designed te provide
a firm foundation for the establishment of the general
rate, notably measures to establish a firmer control over
credit, to help balance the budget, to improve the tax
colleotion system, to improve the raw materials allecation
aystem, and to stabilize wages.
TOP
9
As indicated by its title, the new exohange rate 1s
not to be a definitive rate in the sense that it weuld
not be subject to change if the need arose. The Mission
S
BC/R
envisages that a change in the rate after a reasonable
7
period of time is not only possible but probable, and
CP
that authority should be given SCAp to make ohanges in
the rate within specified limits, without Washington
2148
Rev-
approval

approval, in accordance with the principles of the
Internatienal Monetary Fund as applied to member coun-
tries. The Missien emphasizes the impertance ef the
institutien between now and Ooteber 1 of its recemmend-
ed rather drastic measures for promoting exchange sta-
bility, if the new rate is to be maintained and the
expeoted benefits of its establishment are to be achiev-
ed. It is emphatic in its belief that the time is fully
ripe to establish a general rate and that nathing would
be gained, and muoh lost, by further delay.
General MacArthur's position, based on the memeran-
dum of his Foreign Exchange Board, is, in a word, that
the cumulative impact of the effectuation of the referms
recommended by the Mission to be carried out in the next
three months would produce serious danger of a cellapse
of the Japanese econony. He agrees that these reforms
are a necessary prelude to the establishment of a general
rate, but does not think that the economy could stand
them at this time, and that the setting of a single gen-
eral rate should therefore be indefinitely postponed.
His thesis is that Japanese recovery must proceed much
further than at present before the setting of a general
rate will be feasible. In talks with members of the
Mission, SCAp stafr officials stated that they doubted
whether conditions would improve to a point where setting
of a rate would be praotioable for another two or three
years. General MacArthur continues strongly to maintain,
however, that the military conversion rate must be raised
1mmediately.
▲ meeting was held in Mr. Draper's office Saturday
morning to consider the problems raised by the Missien's
report and SCAP's telegram thereon. Seoretary Royall, Mr.
Draper, members of the Mission and representatives of the
concerned Departments were present. I attended more to
keep informed of developments than as a partioipant in
the discussions. The general consensus was strongly in
favor of the Mission's recommendations, whioh seem te me,
after reading the report and listening to the disoussions,
to be far more logical than the SCAP position. The
"price-pooling" soheme presently in effeot in Japan, where-
by the pooled proceeds of sales of each industry are dis-
tributed to the individual firms purely on the basis of
their respective unit costs, removes all incentive for
efficient produotion and the readjustment of Japanese
industry generally to accord with the werld demand
situation

situatien fer Japanese expert products. Many ether tell-
ing arguments against centinuing the present situatiem and
waiting until recovery is further advanoed befere acting
are given in the Mission's report, whioh seems te me an
excellent one.
A second meeting to consider the preblem is being
held this evening at 8 o'clock in Mr. Draper's effice,
which it is hoped will be attended by Mr. Sproul and
Dr. Feis, as well as by those whe attended the meeting
on Saturday. I am told that the report has already been
submitted by Treasury to the NA0, whieh is actively con-
sidering 1t today and tomorrow. The prinoipal probleme
of conoern to FE, considering that the Missien's recom-
mendations are in general accord with FE's earlier posi-
tion, would seem to be (1) whether we wish to support
what seems to be the general view that General MaoArthur
sheuld be informed that after careful consideration of
his views it has nevertheless been decided to proceed en
the basis of the Mission's recommendations, and that he
will be expected to carry out these recommendations to
the best of his ability; and (2) whether we weuld oen-
sider it desirable to modify this course in any way
because of the possibility that it may cause the present
shaky Ashida Government to fall. It is diffioult to
imagine this Government carrying out the presoribed far-
reaching referms, and the likeliheod 1s that it weuld
deoline even to try, and would simply move aside.
Hy personal view is that neither of the above con-

the adoption of the Missien's report and the dispatoh
of instructions to General MacArthur to carry it out.
The situation is suoh where we oan only hope that General
HacArthur will, like a "good soldier", take the pesitien
that he has expressed his point of view and will now carry
out the Government's decision to the best of his ability.

June 22, 1948
SUBJECT:
Information to IATB members re Exohange Rate for Japan.
For several weeks prior to the departure of the Exohange Rate missien
to Japan, the U.S. led discussion at the IATB on various problems
connected with the establishment of a rate. In the course of the
discussions the U.S. informed the IATB that when the mission was in Japan
it would consult with the FEC missions there with regard to the exahange
rate problems.
As you know, there has been no agreement reached with SCaP as regards
the exchange rate. Further, due to the attitude of SCaP headquarters and
Mr. Sebald, no discussions were held between our mission and the FEC
mission in Japan.
Since this is a question upon which the Us has sought views, sinoe
word will spread that the mission has returned, and since our missien did
not confer with FEC missions in Tokyo, we think effort should be made to
courses of action to propose:
a. Call a meeting of the IATB and have Mr. MoDiarmid outline in
analytical form the various economic factors having a bearing on the
db
establishment of a rate, as the mission saw them. Of course, no con-
clusions would be given since there is as yet no decision.
b. The U.S. member to committee 2 could announce on Thursday
to Committee 2 (most members of which also serve on the IATB) that the
mission has returned but that no final position has been reached; we
would then call in some of the missions (UK, NEI, Australia, China) on
an infermal basis and discuss the problems involved, so that the decisions
when made, would be more palatable.
Do you have views as to which alternative is preferable? May we have
them by Wednesday evening? (June 23).

(1) The State and Treasury representatives objeeted

Dear General Saltzman:
Thank you very much for your kind letter of July 1,
received during my absence on vacation, concerning the report of
the recent technical mission on Japanese foreign exchange problems.
The "good work" of the mission is entirely ascribable to the
quality and competence of its members and the fine spirit and
teamwork that characterized the whole endeavor. You will
appreciate that the real credit for the outcome belongs to those
responsible for selecting the mission's personnel.
7-3048
I should like to take this opportunity to express to you
my very high personal regard for Mr. Jchn McDiarmid's part in the
mission's program. He did more than his share of the work and in
addition contributed much invaluahle background to the mission's dis-
cussions. Working intensively in close relationship tests severely
both good spirit and good judgment. McDiarmid was never wanting in
either of these qualities. The State Department is fortunate in
having the services of such a worthy public servant.
Very sincerely yours,
Palph a.
Ralph A. Young.

=== Page 320 === (confidence 0.98)
TOP SECRET
Ofx
TO
.S
DATE: August 9, 1948
FROM
C. C. Hilliard, O
SUBJECT:
Meeting re Single, General Rate of Exchange for the Japanese Yen.
Reference:
Your letter of July l4 to General Noce, enclosing draft cable to
SCAP.
The following is an outline of the meeting held in Mr. Draper's
conference room at the Pentagon on the morning of July 29. Those present
at the meeting are listed at the end of this memorandum.
Mr. Draper said that the Army Department had been actively considering
further the recommendations of the Young Mission report, in conjunction
with General MacArthur's comments and with opinions, for example, from
Dr. Feis, Mr. Sproul and Mr. Loree. The draft cable referred to above is
in reply to General MacArthur's comments in C-61913 of June 29.
Without specific reference to the draft cable, Mr. Draper raised the
following points for discussion:
1. How drastic would the impact of a single, general rate be on
internal prices in Japan and on the present volume of exports?
2. What cushioning of such impact on exports could and should
be effected through subsidies?
3. The ideas of the group on steps other than the exchange rate, -
for example, on the merits, if any, of an export tax to subsidise
imports?
During the two-hour discussion which then took place, I made note of
8
the following.
Re point 1 above, Mr. Hursey displayed a chart showing the ratios of
948
the values of exports, by groups, such as textiles, from the standpoint of
a yen-dollar conversion factor. He pointed out that the chart indicated an
average conversion factor of 147 yen to the dollar. He estimated that an
exchange rate of 220 yen to the dollar would move about 80% of Japanese
exports at the present price level. Projecting these estimates, he expressed
the opinion that the establishment of a single exchange rate at the level
recommended by the Mission, i.e., 270 yen to 330 yen to the dollar, could
be effected without a violent reaction on exports.
Re point 2, Mr. Stuart pointed out that large subsidies are already in
effect in Japan; that budgetary provision should be made instead of unsound
subsidizing
TOP SECRET
2378

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
TOP SECRET
-2-
subsidising; that sound budgeting, in the opinion of the mission, woild
not raise the tax goal too high; and that the Foreign Trade Account
(Boeki Cho) is now approximately in balance only, as compared with a
more favorable showing needed.
Re point 3, Mr. Southard expressed strong Treasury opposition to an
export tax to subsidise imports, and cited a case with respect to Maxico.
Mr. Draper expressed surprise to find that the single rate was intended
to be applicable to food imports. The point was, however, apparently clear
to all of the others present, from having read the Mission's report.
Mr. Saltsman referred to the proposed cable to SCAP, which it had been
understood would be discussed at the meeting, and pointed out the desirability
and need for proceeding with the utmost promptness in this matter. Mr. Draper
replied, with special reference to the food pricing aspect mentioned above,
that he was unable to concur in the proposed cable without a clearer pictur
on the points he had raised at this meeting.
Mr. Draper asked the Young Mission to prepare further schedules for him
in connection with the above points. It resulted that, preferably without
asking for additional data from the theater, the Mission members would dras
up some new schedules, as nearly as practicable in the form desired. I am
informed, however, that a cable has now been sent by the Army Department
to Tokio for some further figures, pending Mr. Draper's return to Washington
from his absence of the past week.
Those present at the meeting, with Mr. Draper presiding, were as follows:
State Department,
Mr. Saltzman
Mr. Butterworth, FE
Mr. Whitman, OE
Mr. McDiarmid, OE (Young Mission member)
Mr. Hilliard, O
Treasury Department,
Mr. Southard
Mr. Stuart (Mission member)
Department of the Army,
Mr. Draper
Mr. Walker
Lt. Col. Feyereisen (Mission member)
Lt. Col. Smith
Mr. Pollard
Major Archer
Mr. Henser (Mission member)

CIVIL AFFAIRS DIVISION
WASHINGTON 25, D. C.
1 July 1948
Honorable Charles E. Saltzman
Assistant Secretary of State
Department of State
Washington 25, D. C.
Dear Mr. Saltzman:
Your letter of 30 June has been received and brought to the attention
of the Secretary of the Army.
The position taken by the Secretary of the Army at the NAC meeting on
28 June with respect to a commercial rate of exchange for the yen, is well-
known to the State and Treasury Departments. At that meeting the Secretary
of the Army stated he was in agreement with the recommendation of the NAC.
He further mede a statement concerning certain important factors which
must be taken into consideration with those purely economic and financial
matters contained in the report of the Young Mission. Following a brief
discussion after the Secretary of the Army had made his statement, the
Chairman of the NAC, in summarizing the position of NAC members stated
that a general commercial exchange rate should be adopted as soon as
administratively possible and practical.
Fron the Chairman's Summary of the Meeting, it is concluded that
the NAC recognizes fully the other vital factors which must be considered
in implementation of the NAC policy.
Since the meeting, the Department of the Army has received an additional
cable from the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers further emphasizing
the need for careful consideration of these same additional factors brought
to the attention of the NAC by the Secretary of the Army. The Department
of the Army has and is giving careful attention to the Young Report, the
NAC recomnendation, the cable from the Supreme Conunander for the Allied
Powers, and all other pertinent available information in this connection.
Under the circumstances, the Department of the Army cannot concur in
the agreed State-Treasury position set forth in your letter, namely that
the date of 1 October should at this time be accepted as a target date.
for the establishment and commencement of operation of a single general
rate; nor can it now agree to any earlier date. It is accordingly sug-
gested that the representatives of our respective departments further
explore this problem of major importance before arriving at a final U. S.
position.
TOP SECRET
to SCA
1759

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.98)
TOP SECHET
As stated for a long period of time to the other government agencies
concerned, the Department of the Army feels that the present military con-
version rate in Japan is wholly unrealistic and is resulting in great
hardship on all occupation personnel in that theater who are bound by that
rate. Since the introduction of a commercial rate will in any event be
delayed for several months, and in view of the delay of over two months
since the original request by the Theater Commander for relief in this
connection, the Department feels it most reasonable to hold to the agree-
ment of 6 May between the Departments to the effect that a new military
rate would become effective on 1 July.
We ask an immediate conference, prior to 4:00 pm, this date, wherein
the State and Treasury Departments will be consulted as to the level of the
conversion rate in accordance with the 6 May agreement. After such a
consultation the Department of the Army desires to proceed with the grant-
ing of authority to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers to set
such a conversion rate.
I wish to assure you the Department of the Army will continue to
pursue vigorously the subject of a general rate and the attendant financial
and other reforms required under this proposed general program.
Sincerely yours,
Danit
DANIEL NOCE
Major General, GSC
Chief, Civil Affairs Division

Department of State
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Doherty:
While on vacation, from which I returned the first part of this
month, I received word of the change in the rate of exchange for
Japan. Let me express to you the thanks of the organizations
which we represent. This, as I told you in Washington, is
going to be a great boost to us in our work out there and I feel
sure will also react favorably on what the Government is trying
to accomplish in Japan.
Since returning to the office I have been approached by people
who have local currency in Japan who are under the impression
that the regulations now permit us to make arrangements with them
to use their yen and they are ready to help us out in our work in
CS/A
various ways if we are able to use their local currency. I have
contacted our banks to find out whether or not the regulations
would permit this and have been advised by them to write to
Washington to ascertain just how the regulations affect transac-
tions such as this. Let me assure you that we are not interested
in doing anything that is not entirely within the regulations
established by the Government. We appreciate very much what the
894.5151
Government has done and the relief that it has made available to us.
Would you be so good as to let me know what the regulations are, or
have someone write me so that I may know what answer to give these
friends?
SEP
Very sincerely yours

My dear Mr. Ryan:
The receipt is aoknowledged of your letter post-
marked November 6, 1948 concerning the exchange value
and convertibility into U.S. dollars of Japanese yen.
There is no general rate of exchange with foreign
currencies of the Japanese yen and no provisions at pre-
sent exist for converting yen into dollars, with a few
894.5151
exceptions chiefly related to military and wivilian
personnel associated with the Allied occupation of Japan.
Even in these few instances the conversion of yen into
dollars is usually based upon a provious conversion of
dollars into yen. The latter procedure is authorised
for very limited purposes inoluding the payment of
personal expenditures in Japan, but not inoluding the
purchase of goods intended for export from Japan. Imports
and exports are handled through independent yen and
foreign currenoy accounts which are set up in the name of
the Japanese Board of Trade (Boeki Cho).
-948
No information is available as to when Japanese yen
may be freely or relatively freely convertible into
foreign currencies. At present Japan possesses no foreign
exchange assets which could be used for capital transfers,
and it is not probable that such assets will have acerued
to Japan's credit for some time to come.
Sincerely yours,
CH

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
6 January 1949
MEMORANDUM FOR Genoral MoCoy
Subjeots
Disoussion with Mr. G. J. Jobsis (Netherlands Delegation)
Concerning the Procedure to be Followed in Establishing
an Exchange Rate for the Yen
1. On 4 January, Mr. Jobsis called at my office to talk briefly with
me concerning the question of establishing an exchange rate for the yen.
He made it olear that he was not speaking as a member of the Netherlands
Delegation nor indeed as a Dutohman, but rather on a striotly personal
basis. His objective. was, he said, to pass on some advice which he felt
he could not give in any official capacity. He said that he would
appreciate my passing on what he had to say to any interested members of
the U.S. Government for what it might be worth. I summarize his remarks
below.
2. Mr. Jobsis said that he would consider most unwise and even
dangerous any decision by the U.S. Government to permit either SCAP or
the Japanese Government to establish an exchange rate for the yen. He
pointed out that there was no precedent for granting such authority in
ciroumstances suoh as now obtain in Japan. For example, since SCAP or
the Japanese Government (whioh Mr. Jobsis considers to be a direot agent
of SCAP) has ordered the sale of certain Zaibatsu seourities in the yen
market, a decision to revalue the yen--which would almost certainly be
in the direction of devaluation--would put SCAP or the Japanese Government
in the position of being liable for sizable damage suits from those per-
sons whose interests have been injured in the process. It was the further
opinion of Mr. Jobsis that an international court of justice might regard
as highly questionable a procedure whereby the same agenoy could force the
sale of property one day and devalue the currency involved in the sale the
next. In his view, the U.S. Government would not only be establishing an
extremely dangerous precedent, but would call down upon itself severe
criticism from many quarters.
3. When asked what practical alternative he could suggest to estab-
lishment of an exchange rate by either SCAP or the Japanese Government,
he admitted that it would probably be impossible for the Far Eastern
Commission to agree on a rate and that the Inter-Allied Trade Board would
doubtless experience similar difficulty in agreeing on a recommendation.
He did suggest, however, that a possible solution would be to have either
SCAP or the Japanese Government consult with suoh an agenoy as the Inter-
national Monetary Fund. While Japan is, of course, not a member of the
Fund, the entrance of Japan into more or less unrestrioted international
trade injeots the problem of Japanese currency into the world trade
pioture. Since one of the primary functions of the Fund is to stabilize
currencies, Mr. Jobsis felt that no harm could be done by calling on the
Fund for advice. And having called for advice from an international
agency, the curse of "unilateral" aotion would be somewhat mitigated.
4. Mr. Jobsis made a further point of the fact that having onoe
granted to the Japanese Government the right to establish the yen
exchange
4707

=== Page 365 === (confidence 0.93)
CONFIDENTIAL
exchange rate, a precedent will have been established fer permitting
the Japanese Government to alter that rate more or less at will. Ho
felt that any such grant of power to the Japanese Gevernment at this
stage was highly undesirable. It was his view that the U.s. Gevermmest
would itself regret in the long run any such grant of authority to the
Japanese Government.
Daniek.b.ealer
David K. Biohler

=== Page 368 === (confidence 0.99)
SECRET
TO
: FE - Mr. Butterworth
DATB: Apri1 21, 1949
FROM : NA - Mr. BishopW
suject: Placing of Allied Missions in Japan on a Self-Supporting Basis.
1. The Problem
To determine what action should be taken in compliance with SCAP's recom-
mendation (TAB A), supported by the Department of the Army (TAB B), that all
*Allied Missions in Japan be placed on a completely self-supporting basis and
that they be required to purchase their yen currency with acceptable foreign
exchange.
2. Discussion
A U.S. interim directive, dated July 24, 1946 authorized (not directed)
SCAP to advance yen to meet necessary official expenses incurred in Japan by
*Allied Missions and by Allied governments for their occupation forces in Japan.
The directive further stated that SCAF should take appropriate steps to insure
14.515
that excessive amounts of yen are not issued under the above authorization and
are limited to "such amounts as can reasonably be regarded as occupation
expenses." On the basis of this directive SCAf has provided funded yen to
Allied Missions at a monthly rate which has averaged since May 1, 1947 about
6,000 yen per member. U.S. occupation forces, too, have received funded yen,
although SCAP has recently taken steps to eliminate such funding so far as his
forces are concerned.
In addition to the funded yen, the Allied Missions and the Occupation
forces receive on procurement demand, without reimbursement to the Japanese
Government, housing, office space, utilities, maintenance, rail transportation,
etc. The value of the services, supplies and facilities received on a procure-
ment demand basis greatly exceed the value of the funded yen.
In his cable (TAB A), SCAr has proposed that all the Allied Missions, but
not the occupation forces, be "placed on a completely self-supporting basis"
which is taken to mean the elimination for them of all yen funding and procure
ment demands. SCAP has advanced the following arguments: (a) the continuation
of the present system of logistic support for Allied Missions by the Japanese
Government is at variance with the U.S. Interim Directive on Japanese stabili-
zation; (b) since this constitutes a drain on the Japanese economy, there is a
corresponding requirement for increased U.S. relief and rehabilitation funds,
S
and (c) 
*The term "Allied Missions", as used in this paper and its enclosures, refers t
the Tokyo Missions of FEC countries including their Reparations and IMT deleg
tions and to the Soviet Member of the Allied Council and his staff. The term
"occupation forces" refers to all personnel assigneckoOrtveNr SCAP and to t
British Commonwealth Occupation Forces.
SECRET

=== Page 369 === (confidence 0.97)
SECRET
-2-
and (c) it is inequitable to grant financial benefits, chargeable to the
Japanese economy, to the Allied Missions all of which are primarily engaged
"in furthering their own national interests."
3. Recommendations
It is recommended that the attached letter (TAB C) be sent to Mr. Voorhees,
setting forth the Department's views on how this matter should be handled.
While the letter is largely self-explanatory, it should be noted that the
Allied Missions receive only about one-fiftieth of the total amount of free yen,
services, supplies, facilities received by the occupation forces. A real
reduction in occupation costs will therefore require a reduction in the logistic
support of the occupation forces along with that of the Allied Missions.
4. Concurrences:
L and FN have concurred in the position taken in the letter to
Mr. Voorhees. CAD (Army) has informally expressed concurrence.
19
NA:!Green:br

necessary official expenses incurred in Japan by authorized
allied commissions, mission assigned to the SCAP staff, nation-
ai representatives and their staffs on the Allied Council for
Japan, and Allied Governments for their occupation forces in
Japan". The directive further stated that:
A. Yen so advanced be limited to such amounts as
can reasonably be regarded as occupation expenses,
including reasonable salaries or other compensations
to personnel to enable maintenance of reasonable stan-
dard of living in Japan, hire of Japanese or other
local employees and purchase of necessary official
supplies and equipment which 1t is impracticable to
import.
B. SCAP should take appropriate steps to insure
that excessive amounts of yen are not issued under the
above authorization.
In keeping with this directive this headquarters speci-
fied what might be considered legitimate expenses and initially
fixed 4,O00 yen per authorized member of an allied official
agency as the funding limit. On lst May 1947 the limit was
raised to 6,Ooo yen per member. This lim1t has been maintained
notwithstanding persistent and increasing pressure by a number
of the missions for a higher ceiling. The Chinese Mission
almost invariably reguests more than the limit. The Soviet
mission demands funding at the rate of 25,000 yen per member.
The United Kingdom Liaison Mission, whose past demands have

been well within the authorized limit, now dispute the author-

the Allied Missions are furnished on procurement demand, with-
out reembursement to the Japanese Government, housing, office
space, utilities, maintenance and maintenance staffs, and rail
transportation on official business.
At the present the monthly yen funding to the Allied
Missions approximate 3.5 million yen while the total funding
to date approximates 92.5 million yen. The monthly cost to
the. Japanese Government of the supplies, services and facili-
ties furnished on a non-reimbursable procurement demand basis
approximates 47.4 million yen while the total of such costs
to date approximates 1.8 billion yen computed at the present
yen rate.
In view of the indefinite postponement of a peace trea-
ty for Japan and the present emphasis on the stabilization of
the Japanese economy, as expressed in radio W·81058, the
continuing reguirement that Japan furnish logistic support for
these missions is inconsistent with the provisions of that
directive and can no longer be justified. These missions have
long since accomplished their contributions to the occupation
and except for the purely perfunctory activities of the Allied
Council for Japan, their functions now follow the pattern of
those common to non-allied missions or agencies. They are
concerned primarily with the conservation of commercial and
political interest of their respective governments and nation-
als. Indeed, there is strong reason to believe that in certain
instances the funded yen have been used for illegitimate and
subversive purposes inim1cal to the best interests of Japan.
If a strict accounting is required, there is no effective means
of verifying their statements or of correcting abuses without
precipitating international controversy.
