In order to reduce the burden on the Japanese budget,
action has been initiated to eliminate all non-reimbursed yen
funding for United States Occupation Forces in Japan. Their
requirements for indigenous services, facilities and materials
will be met entirely by procurement demand in kind or with
purchased yen. Euch precurement demand for the United States
Occupation Forces is carefully scrutinized before being passed
to the Japanese Government for compliance. United States per-
sonnel desiring yen for their personal use are required to
purchase such yen for dollars which are deposited in the SCAP
trust fund to be disbursed for the direct benefit to the
Japanese economy. Similarly, United States personnel pay
dollars for personal services performed by Japanese. While
current instructions reguire all occupation personnel to pay
dcllars or other acceptable currencies for personal service,
the BCOF and missions of the British Commonwealth have thus
far refused to comply. Neither will they agree to pay for
Japanese labor employed in their canteen service on the plea
that this would result in higher prices for commodities sold
there.
There is an obvious ineguity in granting financial
benefits, chargeable to the Japanese economy, to agencies which
contribute ncthing toward the economic rehabilitation of Japan
while they are primarily engaged in furthering their own nation-
al interests. Since their logistic support constitutes a
drain on the Japanese economy, there is a corresponding require-
ment for increased United States relief and rehabilitation
funds. Furthermore, no satisfactory explanation can be advanced
for granting allied personnel benefits which are denied our own
forces.
The deferment of a peace treaty imposes the continuing
burden on Japan for the support of the occupation forces.
Every reasonable step is being taken to reduce our demands to
the minimum consistent with efficient operation, including the
cessation of construction of sorely needed dependent housing.
The Japanese have been directed to accomplish stringent curtail-

The Secretary of State
Dear Mr. Secretary:
DEPARTMENT OF STATE 
The Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers has directed atten-
tion to a situation of increasing gravity under which the United
Kingdom Liaison Mission is disputing the authority of the Suprame
Commander to set a limit on yen advances to accredited Allied
894.5151/1-2849
Missions in Japan for the payment of necessary official expenses.
A directive of 23 July 1946 authorized SCAP to limit advances
to such amounts as can reasonably be regarded as occupation expenses,
including reasonable salary or other compensation to personnel to
enable maintenance of a reasonable standard of living in Japan, hire
of Japanese or other local employees, and purchase of necessary
official supplies and equipment which it is impracticable to import.
y
SCAP was also directed to take appropriate steps to insure that
excessive amounts of yen are not issued. Accordingly, SCAP estab-
lished a monthly rate of 4,000 yen per authorized member of Allied
Missions as the funding limit for legitimate expenses, and on 1 May
1947 paised the limit to 6,000 yen per member. This limit has been
maintained despite demands for higher ceilings from the Chinese and
Sovioo Missions. The U. K. Liaison Mission which has kept well within
the limits in the past is now requesting funds in excess of the 6,000
yen ceiling,
SCAP ponts out that in addition to the funded yen the Allied
Misstons recive on procurement demand, without reimbursement to the
Japanese Government, housing, office space, utilities, maintenance,
MAY 16 194º
and rail transportation on official business. The monthly cost is
estimated at 3.5 million yen for funding and 47.4 million yen for non-
FILED
reimbursable services to the Allied Missions, constituting a serious
drain on the Japanese economy and a further pressure for American
relief and rehabilitation funds.
Since the missions have completed their major contribution to
the occupation and are now concerned primarily with the conservation of
commercial and political interests of their governments, the continuing
requirement that Japan furnish logistical support for the missions is
inconsistent with the recent directive to SCAP requesting immediate
enactment of an economic stabilization program for Japan.
The U. S. accredited personnel and quasi-official organizations
18049
1131
2
SECRET
849

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.98)
SECRL
have been required to pay dollars for personal services performed by
Japanese since June 1948. In view of the increased stringencies
imposed upon the Japanese Government to encourage economic stabilisa-
tion and self-support, it is imperative that the inequity of granting
financial benefits to foreign agencies, not responsible for nor
encumbered with the cost of Japanese economic rehabilitation, be
discontinued.
The Department of the Army supports SCAP in his recommendation
and it is requested that action be taken on a governmental level to
place all Allied Missions in Japan on a completely self-supporting
basis and require their purchase of yen currency with acceptable
foreign exchange.
Sincerely yours,
Kenneth C. Royall
Secretary of the Army

Dear Mr Swayze:
On Friday, March 4, 1949 I arrived in New Smyrna Beach
Florida to do some studying in the law offices of Mr W. R.
Clements and upon that date wrote to Mr Donald Kennedy, Chief
International Resources Division of the Department of State,
to advise him of my removal from Grand Rapids, Michigan and
to ask permission to use my disoretion in the matter of
discussion of my government affairs with Mr Clements.
I think that I should explain before going further, that
my office was first established during the war years under
the supervision and guidance of Mr Clements, and with the
permission of the Office of War Information. Since then there
have been numerous occasions when the legal advice of Mr
Clements was necessary to clarify certain points of law
governing banking procedure and export problems at which
times I represented various interests both here and abroad.
CS/A
Some months ago, as you probably remember, a Presidential
Order was published in the Federal Register, regarding papers
that were confidential, and at that time I wrote to Mr Kennedy
who had advised me from time to time as to procedure, sayingo
that I would so regard my papers until such time as I heard 
FILED
from him to the contrary.
4 1949
In effect, this procedure might constitute a stay of
proceedings, which in some instances might be regarded as
an obstruotion of justice, as it might have been in the case
of the Bank of New Smyrna, of New Smyrna Beach, Florida whom
I represented in argument in a procedure of some New York
Banks, whom they, the Bank of New Smyrna, claimed were operating
in restraint of trade.
034305
CA5T

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.99)
March 15, 1949
Mr John M. Swayze
pg 2
I believe that I won my point in conjunction with the matter
and now things are proceeding satisfactorily.
It would now seem, due to the Presidential Order, that I
would have to have permission of the Secretary of which ever
department the matter in question was related to enter into
argument or discussion here. This may or may not have been
improperly interpreted by me, however, I feel that in order to
have the proper leave to cooperate in these legal matters
I should have permission from the Department of State.
I have had no reply from Mr Kennedy, and expect that I
should have addressed you first in the matter. No doubt the
question would have been referred to you eventually. I am
anxious to get on with some discussions of banking and foreign
trade and am hoping that the proper permission to do so will
soon be forthcoming.
Trusting that I will hear from you in the very near future
I am,
Sincerely

TAR

TREAS
General Headguarters a plan for handling the importation of ma-
FRB
terials into Japan to be purchased with the proceeds of Japanese
exports.
According to a representative of the Foreign Trade and Com-
merce Division, Economic and Scientific Section (Ess) of this
Headquarters, issuance of this SCAPIN to the Japanese Government
paves the way for greater Japanese control over imports to be
purchased with the roceeds of Japanese exports. No time limit
is mentioned in the SCAPIN but the requirement to the effect that
import programs be prepared for each uarter of the Japanese fis
cal year commencing April 1, 1949, suggests that relatively prompt
action on the part of the Japanese authorities is expected.
Assuming that the Japanese Government submits an import plan
that will be approved by this Headquarters, it is expected by ESS
that the provisions for control of foreign exchange specified in
SCAPIN 1968 will be carried out in part by permitting the newly
established Foreign Exchange Control Eoard to tko over much of
the control work now being performed by the Funds Control Division
of Ess. Another possible development envisaged by ESs is the grant-
ing of permission to Japanese private firms to import an appreciable
share of imports purchased by means of proceeds from expegts.

licenses.

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.99)
COSMIC
ART
A. A REQUEST TO YOU:
for information concerning notable examples of modern religious-
metaphysical art for illustrations in a comprehensive book on COSMIO ART
Examples may come from any country, from any religion, Bast or West, pro»
duced since 1917. Summary of Theme: The story of man's perilous pil-
grimage in a mysterious divine universe in search of security and self-
fulfillment, here and hereafter, as portrayed in novel forms of recent art.
"All great inspired art becomes the revelation
Of the living Harmony and Order of the cosmos."
From Wake of Beauty, a poem in Golden Benediction by Ruth Harwood.
B. ARTISTIC QUALITIES DESIRED:
"It is all triumphant art, but art in obedience to laws."
From the poem, Abt Vogler, by Robert Browning.
Originality of imagination; fine design and craftsmanship; serious, ideal-
istic motivation; deep human appeal or value; universality of esthetio
principles; form and color exactly expressing content.
Other pertinent qualities: essentialized, ideographic, symbolic,
simplified, abstract, dynamic, expressionistic;
not conventional, orthodox, sentimental, imitative, or trite.
.C. ARTS INCLUDED:
All space arts: painting, prints, etc., sculpture, architecture, stained
glass, ceramic arts, ritual objects, photography.
Some time arts:
the dance (in photographs), rites, metaphysical poetry.
D. CHAPTERS, WITH CHIEF TOPICS:
(underlining means of primary importance)
1. COSMIC BEGINNINGS: chaos, creation, order; light, sea, firmament; time,
space; mother-earth, evolution of earth, life, man; birth of the soul.
2. STREAM OF HUMAN LIFE: first man, marriage, birth, children, madonnas.
3. DEMONIC ADVERSARIES: fear, suffering, finitude; war, bomb, tragedy,
devils, death, crucifixion; escape to freedom; from darkness to light.
HEROES OF HOPE AND COMPASSION: saints, Prophete, saviore, incarnation;
redemption; power of love and sacrifice; aspiration, faith, hope, ideals;
brotherhood, peace, new world, vision.
5. MAN'S QUEST FOR GOD: primitive religion; God, the holy, etc.; praver, joy;
illumination, revelation, adoration, awakening, levels, cosmic security;
conversion; contemplation, union with God, peace of mind, mystic ecstasy.
6. GOD IN NATURE: divine geometry in nature's designs; snowflakes, orystals;
the sublime in space, time, and power; the immanence of God.
7. TEMPLES AND RITES OF WORSHIP: artistic interpretations of temples; new
religious architecture; ritual objects; worship ceremonies; the univer-
sal priest; decorative symbols (infinite, etc.); sacred dances; windows.
8. INTIMATIONS OF THE BEYOND: immortality, transition, resurrection,
karma, reincarnation; masters, spirits, angela; hell, heaven, Nirvana.
9. COSMIC VISION: metaphysical perspectives; the mysterious; world onenesg;
symbols of the infinite; glimpses into the future; eternity.
E. THANKS FOR SENDING
your suggestions of artists (with addresses), names of art works,
photographs, etc., to Dr. R. F. Piper, Dept. of Philosophy,
Syracuse University, Syracuse 1O, N. Y., U. S. A.

The receipt is acknowledged of your letter dated
Maroh 22, 1949, concerning the possibility of remitting
funds to a Japanese citizen in Kyoto, Japan.
Under certain conditions remittances suoh as you
contemplate are permitted by the Supreme Commander for
the Allied Powers. Such transactions are usually under-
taken by American banks, their affiliates or correspondents
which have branch offices in Japan. Among American banks
so represented in Japan are the National City Bank and
the Chase National Bank of New York City and the Bank
of America of California. No doubt there are national
banks in your community which have correspondent relations
with one or more of the named banks and are prepared to
undertake the tranamittal of funds to Japan as permitted.
You may be aware that no Japanese citisen is per-
mitted at present to possess foreign curreney instruments,
so that if no objections are raised about the tranafer of
funds you contemplate, Mr. Insho of Kyoto would in due
course zeceive Japanese yen funds possibly at the military
conversion rate of 270 yen to one U.S. dollar, sinee there
is at present no other authorized remittance rate.

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.99)
National Advisory Council
Staff Draft No. 286
March 25, 1949
MEMORANDUM TO: National Advisory Council
FROM: National Advisory Council Staff Committee
SUBJECT: Proposed Single Rate of Exchange for Japanese Yen and
Related Measures.
1. The Problem
SCAP has requested authorization to establish a general commercial
exchange rate for the Japanese yen on April 1, 1949, or as soon thereafter
as practicable at 330 Japanese yen to 1 U.S. dollar. Discretionary
authority is also requested to adjust this rate within a range of 10 per-
cent above or below this rate without further consultation. The request
indicates that the rate would apply to permitted foreign trade and exchange
transactions including transactions for which the military conversion
rate is now applicable, as a basis for accounting for official expenditures
of the occupation forces, and for payment of personal expenditures of
occupation personnel and others. The request has the concurrence of
Mr. Joseph Dodge, Financial Adviser to SCAP.
In connection with the introduction of the proposed rate of exchange,
SCAP indicates that no export subsidies are to be applied, but that to
maintain domestic price stability and support the proposed rate, it is con-
sidered necessary to continue import subsidies, at a reduced rate, on about
half the programmed imports for the Japanese fiscal year 1950 (commencing
April 1, 1949). 63 percent of the expenditures on subsidies would be on
food and fertilizer imports, and the remainder mainly on coking coal, pig
iron and iron ore imports.
It is indicated that a yen counterpart fund, based on the dollar
cost of United States aid supplied and converted at the general exchange
rate will be established.
2. Background
On June 28, 1948 the National Advisory Council adopted an action
favoring the establisnment as soon as administratively possible of a single
general exchange rate for the yen applicable to all types of transactions
including those for which a military conversion rate would be used. The
Council perceived no reason to object to a level between 270 yen and 330 yen
per dollar if established within a time limit envisaged in the report of the
special mission on yen foreign exchange policy.
The proposal of SCAP to establish a general commercial exchange rate
on April 1, 1949 is not accompanied by statistical or other data which
would provide an adequate basis for appraisal of the appropriateness of
DECLASSIFIED
TOP-SEGRET
319054
NACActi0n76-303l1lmay76)
By. Z.H6. NARS, Dale 3.LLy'78

=== Page 397 === (confidence 0.99)
National Advisory Council
Staff Draft No. 286
-2-
the level now proposed. Such information as is available to constituent
agencies of the National Advisory Council conceming economic and budget
trends through 1948 would strongly suggest, however, that the rate level
proposed for establishment on April 1 overvalues the yen as compared with
the range of rates proposed in June 1948 by the special mission on yen
foreign exchange policy for introduction as early as August 15 and not
later than October 1. There has been a substantial further inflationary
development since October 1; although black market prices appear to have
become stabilized at a high level, wage rates have continued to rise.
The mission recommended that the final rate selected within its suggested
range should provide for some cushion to allow for future increases in
the domestic price level in Japan.
The rate now proposed for introduction on April 1, 1949 would not
appear to provide any significant margin against further domestic price
increases and would appear to require very considerable and rapid adjust-
ments in unit production costsof export commodities if Japanese exports
are to be sustained and increased in accordance with present plans.
However, SCAP estimates that on the basis of December prices, roughly
80 percent by value of the Japanese exports programmed for the fiscal year
commencing April 1, 1949 can move at the proposed rate provided specified
adjustments are made. Producers of the remaining 20 percent of programmed
exports would be required to reduce costs and profits to enable them to
export at this level. SCAP argues that an exchange rate for the yen higher
than 330 to the dollar either will disturb the existing price and wage
structure or, if offset by increased subsidies, will unbalance the Govem-
ment's budget which is now expected to be held in balance during the
coming fiscal year.
3. Local Currency Counterpart Fund
While the SCAP proposal indicates that it is planned to establish a
local currency counterpart fund equivalent to the value of United States
aid based on the dollar cost of such aid converted at the exchange rate
to be established, information now available in Washington does not
adequately show how the fund would be administered. Accordingly, this
aspect of SCAP's proposal is not presented for Council consideration at
this time.
4. Discussion
The establishment of a single general rate of exchange for the yen
applicable to permitted foreign trade and exchange transactions including
transactions for which the military conversion rate is now applicable, as
a basis for accounting for official expenditures of therforces, and for
payment of personal expenditures of occupation personnel and others is a
stated objective of United States Govermment foreign financial policy.
TOP SEGRET

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.99)
National Advisory Council
Staff Draft No. 286
-3-
While the available evidence may suggest that the level of the single
rate proposed may overvalue the yen to a degree which will require sub-
sequent modification in the interest of achieving SCAP's foreign trade
objectives, it appears that this rate may permit the achievement of
planned export volume for the Japanese fiscal year 1950 if present plans
to achieve economic and financial stabilization in Japan are successful.
SCAP has requested that approval of the proposed rate level include
discretionary authorization to adjust the initial rate 10 percent up or
down without further consultation. In the event that adjustment is
required subsequent to the establishment of an initial rate, the possibil-
ity exists that an adjustment in excess of 10 percent may be necessary.
It would appear desirable to minimize the frequency of possible future
adjustments in the Japanese exchange rate and to prcvide for review by
the National Advisory Council of the circumstances giving rise to any
subsequent decisions to do so. However, on the basis of available informa-
tion, there would appear to be no grounds for objection if, upon review,
ScAP were to determine that the rate initially to be set should be on the
order of 10 percent higher than that proposed.
5. Action
The Staff Committee submits the following action for the considera-
tion of the Oouncil:
The National Advisory Council advises the Departments of State
and the Army that:
(a) Establishment of a single general exchange rate for the yen
applicable to permitted foreign trade and exchange transactions including
a basis for accounting for official expenditures of therforces, and for
payment of personal expenditures of occupation personnel and others is
appropriate and consistent with the foreign financial policy of the
lishment of a rate between 330 yen and 360 yen per dollar on April 1,
1949 or as soon thereafter as may be preetioable.
TOP SECRET

I am afraid the Army has thrown us a fast one from left
field again. You will recall the draft which you approved
Tuesday night, March 29, regarding the establishment of an
exchange rate in Japan (Tab A). That message was not sent, and
in its place there was sent the attached personal cable from

Major Eklund brought over this morning a draft cable from
SAOAS (for State and Army Departments) for SCAP (Tab C). With
Mr. Allison's approval I told Major Eklund we would clear the
cable if they would add the sentence which is pencilled in:
"State continues to support strongly NAC recommendation expressed
in second sentence paragraph (b) of NAC resolution."
I do not understand MacArthur's motivation in asking for
"authorization" to establish the exchange rate. Unless he is
willing to recognize, which he does on occasion, the directive
force of United States policy, "directives" in the technical and
internationally agreed sense of the term can be sent to him only
in the fcrm of a "directive" containing the substance of an
FEC policy decision or in the form or an "interim directive"
from the United States Government on a matter or urgency. In
this sense General MacArthur was already given full authoriza-
tion in the interim directive on economic stabilization.

worry that Japanese economy would become deflated as
a result of the exeoution of the new budget are not awire
of the powerful influence which this fund will exert in
the manipulation of the monetary market.":

mitting proposals for intended fund expenditures should conside?

aspecially the debt held by the Bank of Japan and ther benking in-
stitutions, end also coordinate this action vith legitimate needs
of. private and public credit.

otherwise available from either normal overnment ry nues, exist-
ing credit sources, or the savings of the Japanese peopls.
Proposals calling for Counterpart Fund advance: to private
and public investments will be considered in the i ht of chiey -

and economic stabilization.

The reaction of Japanese officials to the announcement of
the new rate was summarized April 24 by the Nippon Times. English-
language daily published at Tokyo, in an article which stated
that "the linking of Japan's productive structure to international
economy was stressed by the Government in a statement on the es-
tablishment of the single exchange rate as the only means of bring-
ing about the stability of national livelihood, complete employment
of the population and the achievement of a self-supporting economy
for Japan. Calling for the wholehearted cooperation of the nation,
the Government promised every measure to lead this step to success."
Continuing, the Nippon Times reported the views of key Japanese of-
3/
ficials as indicated in the enclosed copy of the main part of the
April 24 news item. While the reactions were generally favorable,
the officials in question reportedly pointed to the need for cer-
tain adjustments, for instance, in the commodity price structure,
to take account of the new rate.
According to a survey of editorial ccmment contained in the
April 24 issues of Tokyo vernaculars, prepared by the Allied Trans-
lator and Interpreter Section (ATIS), General Headquarters, Jap-
anese Government officials and businessmen generally welcomed the
United States decision to fix the single exchange rate at 360 yen
to the dollar. Although surprised at the new rate--most of them
had expected it to be pegged at 330 to one--they regarded it as a
reasonable first step toward meshing the domestic economy with that
of the world. Most of them concurred with a business spokesman who
termed the revalued yen "an appropriately dear yen." But with the
new rate taking effect April 25, many export industries previously
dependent on a yen-dollar exchange of 400 to one will either have to
pare production costs or shut down. Key industries in the rationalize-
cr-die category include shipbuilding, raw silk, sundry &vods, and
aluminum.
According to the Asahi Shimbun, the Commerce and Industry
Ministry predicted that the 360-1 rate would give Japan a good
chance of attaining 70 to 75 percent of the 600,000,000 dollar
export. program for the 1949 fiscal year. Meanwhile, it may be
noted, the Nihcn Keizai Shimbun quoted Commerce-Industry Minister
Heitaro

MEMORANDUM FOR: . JAPANESE GOVERNIENT
SUBJECT:
Establishment of Official Exchange Rate for
Japanese Yen
1. The Japanese Government is directed to take the steps
necessary to put jrtc effect at 0001 hours, 25 April 1949, an of-
ficial foreign exchange rate of 360 Japanese yen to one U. S. dol-
lar. Rates for other currencies will be based on this rate trans-
lated into the U. S. dollar values of such currencies as registered
with the International. Monetary Fund. This rate will be the basis
for all permitted foreign trade and exchange transactions, including
transactions for which military conversion rate is now applicable,
as well as deposits to the United States Aid Counterpart Fund for
Japanese Stabilization.
2. Effective 0001 hours, 25 April 1949, foreign trade and
exchange transactions will for the present continue to be executed
through the Board of Trade at the above rate and in accordance with
existing approved procedures. It is desired that the Japanese Gov-
ernment submit for approval of General Headquarters, Supreme Com-
mander for the Allied Powers, at the earliest practicable date,
recommended arrangements whereby transactions may be executed on
the basis of this exchange rate through the Foreign Exchange Con-
trol Board.
3. Except as otherwise provided herein, export contracts which
were approved by the Japanese Government prior to 25 April 1949 will
be honored in accordance with yen payment terms which were agreed
upon between the Japanese Government and exporters of Japar ase goods
under then extstent export procedures. Any such contract now in
force which requires use of imported raw materials for the execution
thereof shall be discharged.
a. At yen prices specified under then existent export
procedures to the extent that imported raw materials procured from
the panese Government were paid for at yen prices effective at the
time of approval of the contract.
b. At
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. 1 to
Apr11.29, 1949.
b. At adjusted yen prices nly to the extent that im
ported raw materials actually used for roduction of goods to b
exported under the contract are procured fron the Japanese Govern-
ment and are paid for at yen prices in excess of those effective at
the time of approval of the contract. Applicaticns for such ad-
justments will be subject to review and approval by the Price Board.
4. The Japanese Government is hereby directed to take actions
necessary to insure that exporters of Japanese goods fulfill all
contracts approved prior to 25 April 1949 in good faith and in ac-
cordance with the provision: f this memorandum.
5. Direct communication between interested staff sections of
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers and appropriate agencies
of the Japanese Government is authorized to facilitate implementation
of this memorandum.
FOR THE SUPREME COMMANDER:
R. M. LEVY,
Colonel, AGD,
Adjutant General.

ANNOUNCE YEN RATE
General HacArtbur today directed the Japanese government to
adopi, an official foreign exchange rate of 360 en to one U. S.
dollar, to take effect April 25, and to pply to all permitted
foreign trade and exchange transactions, including transactions
for which the military conversion vete is now applicable.
Rates for other currencies will be based upon the official
U. S. dollar values cf such currencies as registered with the Inter-
national Monetary Fund translated inte the yen-dollar ratie.
The directive to the Japanese overment provides that for
the time being foreign trade and exahange transac ions will be con-
tinued through the Japanese Beard  f Trads at the fficial rate and
under existing trade and control proc edures. However, the Japanese
tovernment is inst ructed to suimit plans at the earliest practicable
date which will permit foreign exchange trensactions to be executed
through the Foreign Exchange Control Doard.
To preclude any intercuptlone of transactlons to be implesented
under expert contracts which were appreved prier to the effective
date of the officlal rate, the Japulese government las been directed
to take actions necessary te insure that the exporters of Japanese
goods fulfill all such contracts in good faith and in accordsnce
with the provisions of the exchange directive.
Export contracts now in force which were approved prior to
April 25, 1949 and witi ch require the uss of iaported raw materials
for the execution thereef, are te be discharged in accordance with
the tenas of yen paynents previousl ar enged between the Japanese
government and the expertere of Japanese soods, exeept that heces-
sary adjustments of yen paymtents may be made only to the extent
that imported raw materials actuelly used for produscion of goods
to be exported under the contracts are precured from the Japanese

UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to

Apri1.29, 1949.
-2-
The adoption of an official exchange rate is a major step in
the implementation of the Nine-Point Eccnomic Stabilization Pro-
gram which was cutlined by the Supreme Commander for the Ailied
Powers in his letter of Dec. 19, 1943 to the Prime Minister. The
establishment of a rate has been eagerly awaited by both private
and government circles, and its announcement at this time reflects
progress made by the Japanese towards economic stavilization, in-
cluding recent Diet action on the Japanese fiscal year 1949-50
budget.
It is anticipated that the new exchange rate will not only
serve as a significant factcr toward the further normalization of
the conduct of foraign trade but additionally serve as a stimulant
to the current programs designed to rationalize Japanese industries.
The establishment of an exchange rate does not change existing
restrictions upon conversion of yen to foreign currencies or upon
the holding of foreign currencies. The sale of foreign exchange
for. yen can be consummated only tirough foreign exchange controls
now being formulated.

NEW SINGLE RATE HELD FAVORADLE TO EXPORT TRADE
Redoubled Effort to Place Industr in Efficient Basis is Urged
All circles lhailed the establishtent of the single exchange
rate as Iaving a favorable effect upon the export trade, ty provid-
ing a definite challenge t: industrial s to streanline the nation's
productive structure.
Representing Goverrment sircLes, Sommerce-Industry Fanlster
Heitaro Inagaki stressed the need for redoubled efforts to place
Japanese industry on an efficient basis. Commerce-Industry Vice-
Minister Taro Matsuda stated that the new rate would make possible
the much anticipated disinflationary trend in Japanese economy.
Finance Minister Hayato Ikuda was reported as viewing a partlel
revision of the commodity price structure necessary pnd was soid
to be contemplating consultations with ht on this isste.
Represent ing political cireles, Sesretary-General Kezen Hiro-
kawa of the Democratis-Liberal Party welcomed the new rats as wel-
come and appropriate. Shigeru Hori of the Demceratic Farty (coali-
tion) bailed the new rat, as marking tre first step in the ratnm of
Japan into the field of intormational trade.
Kanichi Moroi, managing directer of the Federation of Beoncric
Organizations, pointed out that the effects of the utified rate
would be different depending on industries but the rate was on about
the level anticipated, so that no sharp blow was likely.
Yoshiro Koike, managing director of the Yamcichi Securities
Company, said that with the rate fixed 30 easier than expected the
effects were likely to be favoratle on the stock market. Late Sat-
urday morning re.crts on the stock market indicated that export is-
sues made spurts in view of the "inside" reports of the 3ó0 to one
dollar rate. Little surprise was expressed among financial cireles
at the new rate. Governor Hisato Ichinade of the Bank of Japan,
saw no need for a recompilation of the budget nor a chang. in the
commodity price structure.
Skilful use of import subsidies, greater rationalization of
industrial management, the acceleration of the export trade, and
economy of national consumption thirough a life of austerity were
listed as the keys to the attainment of a balanced international ac-
count for Japan.
Sozaburo Chigira, President of the Japan Eankers! Association,
expressed little concern over the impact of the new exchange rate
on currency, exports and government finances.

805 OF GOAL FIXED FOR EXPORT TRADE MAY BE ATTAINED
New - Rate Will Boost The Tourist Trade, E3B Believes
The cononic Stabilization Board estimated that 80 percent
of the export soal of 600,339,000 for the carrent fiseal year
will be realized under the new exchange rase of 36o te $1.
On the other hand, a bi boost of the tourist trade was seen,
vi th estinated tourist spending during the year placed at 1,072,780
o ¥380,000,000 in Japanese currency. At the originally expected
exchange rate at the level of ¥330 to the dollar, the SB had esti-
mated that 53.3 per cent of the current fis al year export goal
would be realized. The ESB had also calculated that. with an ex-
change of ¥300, 44.1 per cent and at ¥350, £1.4 per cen': of the
goal would be realized.
Under the new exchange rate exports were expected te fall
short of the £oal by 123,c00, 000. The major part of this would
be made up of raw silk.exports, which were placed at ¥37,000,000
or 6.2 per cent of the entire total.
Second on the list of exports to be hard hit were special silk
fabries, silk and rayon lace, knitted goods and benpen materials.
Exports of these commodities were expected to reach $20,000,000
or 3.3 per cent of the planned goal.
The export goal of clhinaware was set at 12,000,000, Lut cer-
tain manufacturers are expected to make the grade through drastie
operational economy.
Toys, glassware, Shristmas deccrations and other sundries,
placed at ¥24,000,000 or 3.9 per cent of the goal, were expected
to be hard hit.
Other items which will also be affected will be shipbuilding
($15,000,000 or 2.5 per cent), chemical products ($14,000,000 or
1.6 per cent and cameras, watches, clocks and binocular: ($5,000,000
or 0.8 per cent).

BUDGET'S REVISION SAID UNNECESSARY
Cabinet Mocts Problems Arising from Fixing of Single  Rate
The establishment of the exchange rate at ¥360 to one dollar
will not necessitate any revisicn of the 1949-50 budget, a Cabinet
spokesman said yesterday.
Chief Cabinet Secretary Kaneshichi Masuda also told reporters
that the Government would not make changes in the present price
structure. He revealed that the Catinet discussed the problems
arising from the fixing of the new exchange rate for one hour yes-
terday morning-
Fellowing reports by Finance Minister Hayato Ikeda and ESB
Director-General Takayoshi Aoki and a discussion of the ¥201,200
million price adjustment fund, the cabinet reached the following
decision:
1
There is no need to revise the price structure
as a whole, although the prices of individual
comnodities not related to the price level might
be changed;
2。
There is some room for adjustment within the
limits of the ¥83,300 million import subsidy
and other subsidies totalling ¥1l7,900 million,
and price adjustments could be m de within that
framework; and
3. The Government is not intending to draft a bud-
get revision, but the study of the problem will
be continued.
If absclutely necessary, some measure may be taken up at an
extraordinary Diet session expected tc be convened in S ptember,
the Government spokesman said. Mr. Masuda also declared that the
Government hoped to adjust differences existing in the various
Ministries sc that the till on the fable of Fersonnel Organization
could be sent to the Diet "within two or three days." Dr. Kiyoshi
Asai, head of the National Personnel Authority, in the meantime,
revealed that the NPA would decide within the week whether or
not it should take a hand in the Governtent:s personnel reduction
program. Meanwhile, Kochinaga Nakagawa, parliamentary deputy director
of the conomic Stabilization Board, indicated that the new 360 to
one dollar exchange rate would necessitate a 14,Ol4 million in-
crease in import subsidies.

BRITISH BANKS IN JAPAN
DGR
The two British banks at present open in Jape
operate under licences which preclude them from accepting
yen accounts, whereas in pre-war Japan there was no such
restriction on foreign banks. This Embasay puts formard
the following considerations in the confident hope that
the competent United States authorities will, after review
of the position, be moved to agree to authorise the British
banks to accept yen accounts as before the wars-
4
(1) The grant of these facilities would be generally
800.
favourable to the promotion of trade between
Japan and the sterling area.
6
(2)
The British banks in Japan are making substantial
894.5151/6-149
sterling facilities available to Japan, and it
is reasonable that they should be able to obtain
the yen facilities to participate in the
financing of exports.
(3)
The facilities referred to would be in no way
harmful to Japanese interests. Before the war,
the business of the British banks in Japan was
usually with non-Japanese concerns and individuals
in the country. The British banks have, in fact,
no desire to compete with Japanese banks for the
accounts of the Japanese general public.
193842
(4)
Japan's best hope for the future depends on her
dropping the excessive nationalism of the past,
SEP14 1949
and discrimination against foreign banks is
6
therefore to be deprecated.
and, most important of all,
(5) Foreign traders normally require yen funds
/for

CH
Rov.

PAST AND PHESET OF JAPAN'S HAPONT TRADE
Viey on Emport Trede in 1942
The following paragraphs set ferth the manner in which Japan's business was re-
established after the War and some of the diffioulties and obstacles which were
enceuntered.
1. No credit of Japan toward foreign countries:
The Japanese market attracted little attention of importers in the United
States and other countries because of the unsteadiness of the general situ-
atiom and obscure information available on Japanese items. There were few
foreign traders in Japan. Little information was exohanged, and it was hard
to imagine to what extent losses and damages had been restored. Accordingly.
there was much doubt as to the quality of merchandise produced in Japan.
2. SCAP's Limitation:
The Japanese people at first were at a loss to estimate what restrictions
SCAP was going to place on the Japanese econcmic system. They were uncertain
as to how far reparations and punishment of war crimes would go. They hesitated
to invest in manufacturing or to plan new enterprises.
3. Lack of Matorials, Instability of Cemmodity Prices:
On account of the waste of materials during the war, there existed an extreme
lack of materials in Japanese industry which caused a steady rise of general
commodity prices. At the same time, Japan had been so econcmically isolated
from the outer world since the outbreak of the war that her home price stracture
had been left to pursue its own irregular course. scar, therefore, decided om
an individual exchange rate, per $1.o0, in accordance with erport merohandise,
as follows:
Hemp-cloth ($400), Stesl ship (400), Raw Silk (420), Communicaticm apparatus
(450), Knitted go0ds (400-500), Optical apparatus (420-500), Miscellaneous
textile goods (500-530), Prec1sion instruments (500), Sewing-machine (410),
Automobile (400), Bicycle (600), Electric appliance (550), Rolled copper (380).
ELectric wire (410), Antimony goods (480), Alumimum goods (550). Soaps (400),
Paper (400), Chemical fertiliser (390), Inorganic medicines (410), Photo film
(500), Cellulo1d (500). Rabber fo0ds (520-570). Ceramics (500-550), Glass wares
(600), Emamelled iron wares (600). Ply-wood (370). Fuits (420), Liquid camphor
(500), Mouth-organ (380). Bamboo articles (430). Fountain pen (450). Artificial
pear1 (470), C1garette lighter (500), Toys (550). Foot wear (500), Objects of
art, curios (500-600).
These different exchange rates prevented a link with foreign markets and,
also, complicated exporting procedures.
4. Lack of Knowledge of Foreign Markets:
As foreign importers were not informed, Japanese exporters likewise were not
informed regarding foreign markets.

=== Page 456 === (confidence 0.98)
: 2-
5. Shertage of Industrial Fund:
Industrial funds were scarce becanse of inflaticm and. limitation of
issuance of currencies. Japanese exporters emcountered mach red tape
1n the year of 1947 and the first half of 1948.
In spite of the feragoing, general exporters gradually became familiar with
air cargo shipments, and became aware of the convenience of Morthwest Airlimes'
air cargo service. Thms, day by day, the mumber of shipments imoreased.
We set up a House Card file for Japanese exporters who have obtained
anthorisation for charges collect shipments, since Japanese natiomals are
not allowed to possess U. S. Dollars.
INCREASE OF EXPORTS FROM JAPAN IN LAST HALF OF 1948.
1. Stabilization of Political Condition:
Since the Japanese have reacted favorably to Scap's occupation policy,
economic restrictions are gradually being loosened. At the same time, the
Japanese government was overcoming the confusion of the postwar peried.
In November of 1948, Nr. Yoshida was elected as Premier of the Cabinet.
Japanese domestic problems, such as labor disputes, etc., have been settled,
simplifying exporting procedures, and much progress is being made in the
export business.
2. Individual contract of export allowed:
After the war, Japan was allowed only a government-to-government basis for
export of Japanese merchandise, but cach individual could make contracts
directly with foreign traders.
3. Restrictions gradually loosen:
Restrictions by SCAp on Japanese nationals have gradually become looser.
Japanese business men can secure approval frem SCAp to travel abroad for
business inspection and personal contact, foreign traders are allowed to
enter Japan easier than before, and materials for mamufacturing expert
merchandise are imported in greater volume than before.
4. Exchange rates are acoustomed:
Even though there are different exchange rates on various export merchandise,
exporters have become accustomed to a certain extent to deciding and quoting
f.o.b. prices to the buyers.
REASONS FOR DECREASE OF EXPORT FROM JAPAN IN THE BEGINNING OF 1949.
1. Single General Exchange Rate Established:
There has been some trouble in establishing a general single rate for
Japanese yen as follows:
Because of the wide variations of price ratios as shown in the foregoing list,
indiscriminate application of a general single exchange rate to all expert
and import merchandise will at once give rise to many serious problems. It
is mecessary that wuoh consideration be given to establishing such an exshange
rato.

=== Page 457 === (confidence 0.98)
=3-
Provided that the single rate for yem was fixed at $360.00 to $1.00 .
there would be a sudden standstill ef thess expert goeds which are paid at
the current price ratie, which would further strangle the eaterprise and
result in many jobless people.
Some of the advantages likely to accrue from the establishment of the
single exchange rate are as follews:
a. It will enable Japanese business men to travel abroad and to open
braaches and agencies everseas.
b. It sets a clear basis for figuring ocean freight rates and marine
insurance premiums, thereby making possible an eventual return to a
C.I.F; basis.
c. It will do away with the need of assessing export prices and, therefore.
simplify export procedures.
d. It will stimmlate producers and exporters alike to greater exertion and
indicate the extent to which enterprise shoald be rationalised.
It will be a primer to free trade. On April 25, 1949, a single exshange
rate was fixed by the Japanese government by order of S0AP, at $360.00
to $1.00 US.
We must not lose sight of the fact that, with the exchange rate fixed on a
relatively low value yen basis, the cost of imports will rise in proportion.
To grant subsidies to cover the difference would entail a burden on the
State Treasury and, consequently, mecessitate heavier taxes. It mmst be
remembered, too, that the lower the exchange value of the yen, the lewer the
prices in foreign currencies of our export merchandise and, therefore, the
less revenue in foreign money and the further behind we shall be left in
free price competition in the world markets. The relatively low yen rate
is, therefore, no canse for glee. Be that as it may, the fixing of the
single exchange rate is a matter for congratulation and marks a defimite
step forward from controlled to normal trade.
How will the 360.00 - $1.00 rate affect Japan's export trade? The Meonomio
Stabilisation Board (commonly called ES) estimates that about 64 - 65 percent of
the "planned" exports for 1949 totalling $600 million in round figures, will be
possible at the new rate.
On April 1, provisional conversion rates were revised to a maximam of X425 - $1.00
for export articles exceeding this rate became virtually unerportable. Principal
among them were: (the figures in parentheses are the respective * - $ cenversien
rates)
Antomobiles (* 430) bicyoles (510) radio sets (550) time pieces (450) steel ships
(500) feather and hides (580) dyestuffs (500) celluloid ware (600) bicyele tires
(570) sheet glass (500) porcelain wares (500 bamboo ware (450) art objects (600).
Previously,  February 1, the conversion rate for exports was limited to a
marimam of p600 - $1. Up to then, some articles had enjoyed a rate as high as
£1,o00 - $1. It is surprising that, notwithstanding the outting dowm of the
conversion rate to ¥425 - $1, we have yet to hear of a single case where an
export industry has been given up on that account. The fact reveals how haphasardly
the prices of limes (exportable at S425 - $1) had been fixed. While the survival

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.99)
of these imdustries is due, mo deabt, to an increased supply ef raw materials
and requisite since and to greater erertion on the part of the indastrialists
concerned, there is no desying that their preduction costs were haphasarlly
and irrationally figured and left room for redaction.
And now the majority of the export industries accept even the $360 - $1 rate
without demur! It is inconceivable that the blow from the fixing af the single
rate will be as severe as mathematical figuring shows. It will mot be impessible
to fully achieve the export goal for 1949. The fact remains, however, that
there are limes of export merchandise at present unexportable at the new rates.
The Board of Trade estimates that the new rate will kill 29 percent of the "plammed"
exports for 1949. Chief among the 29 percent were:
Raw silk, ships, bicycles, cable wires, alumimum wares, time pieces,
leather and hides dye-stuffs, sheet glass, celluloid ware, rubberwares,
porcelain wares, lacquerwares, cameras, toys, binoculars, metal tableware,
cloisonne, musical instruments, enamel. (These are very good customers
from the standpoint of air cargo business.)
But some makers of every one of the articles (except raw silk aad ships) will be
able to export at the new rate. They are the ones who, having rationalised and
attained a high rate of operation, are or will be in the mear future n a paying
basis. Hot all of them are large businesses but the new rate hits mediwm and
small industrialists the harder. This points to three probabilities:
1. Lines unexportable at the new rate will be mostly those made wholly
or partially by medium and small industries.
2. Big enterprises will be better able to stand increased costs of
imported raw materials.
3.
Consequently, financially weak medium and small people will sink under
the weight of competition from big industries.
But the export industry of Japan consists, and has always consisted, for the
most part, of medium and small entreprenueurs. One economic expert says that
95 percent of the exports from Japan in l934 were manufactured goods, of which 63
percent were supplied by medium and small people. These figures may be open to
question, but it is undeniable that our export industry is made up, to a very
great extent, of medium and small industrialists. The fact is clear from a survey
by the Board of Hedium and Small Enterprises, which, citing 1947 as an example
gives the foregoing illustration of the importance of these people to expert
industry:
1. of the exports in that year, those made or supplied wholly at almost
wholly by medium and small industrialists were: Hosiery wear, towels,
blankets, tableoloths, and other fiber goods, leatherware, fruits,
Fungus, menthol, camphor and other produce lines, aquatic preducts in-
cluding agar-agar, fruits, furs, triniets, glassware, tableware, orma-
mental porcelainware, Japanese paper, builders tools, lacquerware,
ivory ware, foils, antimony ware, imitation pearls, moking outfits,
art objects, bamboo products such as fishing tackle, fans and paper
lanterns, midget electric bulbs, toys, artificial flowers, Ohristmas
goods, pencils, sport goods, brushes, tools.

=== Page 459 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
2. Lizes made er supplied almost wholly or largely by mediwm mmd mall
industries:
Fish eil, refrigerated fish, wood ware, foodstuffs including easned
goods, emamel wares, farm toels, alumimumware, tea, celluloid ware,
silk and rayon tertiles, bicycle parts, cameras, bineculars, electrie
bulbs and wires, high-grade cotton piece goods, woolen textiles,
porcelain ware, harmonicas and other musical instruments.
SMALL INDUSTRIES' VIEW ON EXPORT.
Their future will have a very important bearing on the future of Japan's expert
trade as a whole. But they are faced by a grave crisis today.
1. The pressure of competition from big industries.
2. Lack of financial resources.
3. Difficulty of getting bank loans.
4. Government policy of giving priority to big industries in the
supply of raw materials.
It is makeshift and may eventually compel recourse to "cheap laber", mass
dismissal, conversion to domestic imdustries or closing doum. It seems that
small industrialists are at the end of their tether to rationalise in such a
way as to improve technique and equipment, reduce costs and raise wages.
GEERAL VIEW ON TODAY'S JAPAN EXPORT
Now that Japan is thrown into direct contact with world economy, it behooves her
to remedy speedily these fundamental defects in her industrial set-up. Whether
the remedy should be effected in a capitalistic er socialistic way or through a
compromise between the two is a matter for decision in the future. What really
matters is the status of our export trade six months or a year hence. Japam's
economic future will be decided by this question. The answer lies more in
external than domestic situations. To be definite, the vital question is whether
we can secure markets for Japan's export.
1. The world markets are getting smaller and maller for Japan, with
production abroad rising.
2. Increasing restrictions being put on imports.
3. The industrialization of backward countries progressing.
4. Living standard in Baropean countries being lowered.
5. Then there is a world-wide shortage of dollars for which there is little
hope of an early solution.
6. Decrease of commodity prices in New York had affected Japanese export.
7. Instability of political situation in China.

=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
FUTURE SUDARY OF JAPANEBE EXPORT.
1. The United States Army mathorities are credited with the intentiom
of increasing Japan's exports for 1949 to $550 million, twice last
year's or more.
2.
The mitigation of Japan's reparations obligations was announced by sCAP.
We, at present, have to wait for decision of Far Eastern Committee, bat
it is needless to say that mach treatment would be highly helpfal to
her economic reconstruction.
3. The MmOA Fund will be increased.
4. The control over Japan's foreign trade will be relared farther.
Fow the present volume of exports is only 10 percent of the average trade volume
between 1930 to 1934, the period which is to be the basis for Japan's pest war
industrial level. The trade is, therefore, far below the goal set in the
five-year industrial rehabilitation program.

COM
TAR
SCAPIN 2o20 in general follows the principles outlined in this
Mission's despatch no. 767. Depending upom the category of commodity
exported, as specified in an anner to the memorandum, the Japamese
exporter will be allowed to retain from 3 to 1o per cent of the
m
foreign exchange proceeds generated by his export. Category I, for
which the exporter can retain only 3 per cent of the fcreign exchange
accrued, includes coal, ships, rolling stock, fertilizers, certain
timber products, bulk metal products, raw silk, staple fiber, yarns,
and certain gray and bleached textiles. Category II, for which 6
per cent of the exchange may be retained, includes a wide variety of
heavy machinery, finished and semi-finished tertile products (general-
ly excluding wearing apparel), certain porcelain products, paper,
tires and tubes, caustic soda, soda ash, portland cament, tea, and
specified iron and steel products. The maximum of l0 per cent may be
retained from exports of light machinery, highly finished textile
products, and a lengthy list of "sundry goods," chemicals and drugs,
and farm and marine products. Although the annexed lists would appear
to cover practically all of Japan's exports, it is provided that
exporters of any commodities not listed must obtain prior ScaP approval
for the retention of exchange.
Foreign exchange retained by Japanese exporters may be used to 
AUG301949
finance commercial representatives sent abroad, to compensate foreign
FILED
agents, to pay for sales promotion campaigns, to finance certain
imports

imports "designed to maximize export production," or to purchase
credit information, samples, trade publications, patent rights, or
the services of technical or engineering consultants, or for other
purposes approved by SCAP.
To make it possible for manufacturers to participate in the
benefits of the program, it is provided that rights to purchase
foreign credits may be transferred once, to "not more than three
persons within the chain of supply."
In general, retention of foreign exchange will be allowed only
in the cases of transactions financed by letters of credit or by
remittances from abroad; credits from transactions concluded under
open account arrangements will be allowed only upon consent of the
other party to the arrangement in question.
The new program is to be applicable to all export transactions
for which bills of lading were issued on or after June 25, the
Japanese Ministry of International Trade snd Industry has decided.
SCAP officials estimate that total credits accruing to Japanese
exporters will be equal to approximately five per cent of the totel
value of exports.

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
APO 500
AG 091.31 (17 Ju1 48) ESS/FTC
24 June 1949
SCAPIN 2020
MEMORANDUM FOR: JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
SUBJECT:
Purchase of Foreign Exchange Credits for the
Purpose of Stimulating Exports
1. Reference is Memorandum for the Japanese Government from
General Headquarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, AG
386.7 (2 Feb 49) ESS/FIN, SCAPIN 1968, 2 February 1949, subject:
Foreign Exchange Controls.
2. It is desired that the Japanese Government submit plans and
procedures not later than 27 June 1949, to permit purchase of foreign
exchange credits by exporters of Japaness goods in accordance with the
principles outlined herein.
3. Exporters of Japanese goods (other than government agencies)
will be permitted to purchase with yen foreign exchange credits re-
sulting from any private export transaction in accordance with the
following allowable percentages and commodity categories:
a. Category I (see Incl 1) - 3%
b. Category II (see Incl 2) - 6%
c. Category III (see Incl 3) - 10%
In cases where a commodity is not listed under categories indicated
above, separate prior approval of the Supreme Commander for the Allied
Powers will be obtained for the right to purchase an appropriate per-
centage of foreign exchange credit resulting from the export thereof.
4. Foreign exchange retention credits may be available only as a
result of transactions financed by Letters of Credit or remittances re-
ceived from outside of Japan. Transactions financed through open accounts
may be a source of such credits only if relative foreign government's
concurrence is obtained and to the extent of such concurrence. Such
retention credits as do result from open account transactions will be
available only for and/or in the country with which the contract has
been made.
5. Foreign

=== Page 466 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 430,
July 5, 1949.
- 2 -
5. Foreign exchange credits purchased by private exporters of
Japanese goods may be used for the following purposes:
a. Travel and living expenses abroad of Japanese or foreign
personnel sent abroad for the purpose of increasing Japanese trade
and/or production.
b. Compensation for foreign agents merchandising Japanese
goods.
c. Payment for sales prômotion expenses.
d. The importation of such industrial raw materials, machinery,
tools, or other items designed tc maximize export production.
e. Purchase of credit information, samples, catalogs, and
other trade publications.
f. Purchase of patent rights and servicas furnished by
foreign technical and engineering consultants.
g. Other purposes as may be specificelly approved by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.
6. The establishment and operation of a system under which foreign
exchange credits in the currency of the exnort contract are allowed
under this memorandum will be implemented by the Foreign Exchange
Control Board in accordance with reference 1 above. Such credits may
be purchased subject to the following controls:
a. Rights of purchase will not be effective beyond a six (6)
months' period.
b. Rights of purchase may be transferred only once and to
not more than three persons within the chain of supply.
c. No foreign currencies or instruments negotiable within
Japan will be made available to any person.
d. Such other safeguards as may be prescribed by the Supreme
Commander for the Allied Powers.
7. Appropriate Japanese Government agencies will be designated to
submit to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers for approval
names of Japanese nationals requesting travel abroad under provisions
of this memorandum subject to the following limitations:
a. The travel requested must be damonstrated to be confined
to trade activities.
b. The individual

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.97)
Enclosure No. 1
to Tokyo's Despatch No. 430,
July 5, 1949.
- 3 -
b. The individual concerned will not be acting as solo
sales or purchasing agents for the Ministry of International Trade
and Industry or for Japanese trade associations.
8. Direct communication among the Ministry of International
Trade and Industry, designated agencies of the Japanese Government
and interested staff sections of the General Headquarters of the
Suprame Commander for the Allied Powers is hereby authorized to
implement the instructions contained in the memorandum.
FOR THE SUPPEME COLMANDER:
R.M. LEVY,

My dear Mr. Agnelli:
Reference is made to your letter of July 26, 1949 inquiring
as to the convertibility into United States dollars of certain
yen funds which you hold in Japan.
As you may know, the Japanese yen is not at present generally
convertible into dollars or other foreign currencies. The acute
foreign exchange shortage which has made it necessary to supplement
Japanese export earnings with large appropriations of United States
894.5151/7-2649
funds has precluded the use of Japanese foreign exchange for any
purposes except to pay for Japan's most essential import require-
ments. The Department of the Army, however, has made certain arrange-
ments under which imports of news and other recrientatiom materials
may be, at least, partially paid for from appropriated funds. I am
not able to state from the information contained in your letter
whether or not you will be eligible for such compensation. It is
suggested, therefore, that you may wish to address a commmication
to Mr. Robert West, Office of the Assistant Secretary of the Army,
setting forth the full details regarding the time and manner of ac-
quisition of the yen balances in question and indicating whether you
are contimuing to accumulate these funds in connection with the our-
rent dissemination of news in Japan.
an
Sincerely yours,

Honorable Sheridan Downey
United States Senator
Senate Office Building
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator:
We have received an Invitation to Bid #BT-49-5859, issued by
the Ministry of International Trade and Industry of the Japanese
Government (under control of S.C.A.P.) requesting bids on 4,000,000
barrels of crude oil, and stipulating "payment will be made in pounds
sterling only". The value of 4,000,000 barrels is about $8,000,000.
We have ascertained that the pounds sterling referred to are
blocked and unconvertible for dollars. In effect, American producers of
crude oil are prevented from biiding upon this large quantity of crude
oil, in spite of the fact that it is American dollars which are sup-
porting Japan, and of greater moment, the fact that it is American dollars
loaned to Great Britain and ot er sterling areas that are being used to
produce the crude oil to be sold to Japan, thus depriving United States
producers of that business.
Crude oil is being produced in the United States greatly in
excess of domestic needs. We need export business. Prices are being
depressed by reason of supply in excess of demand.
Stripper wells and small producers are shut down for the
same reason.
The bid above referred to closes October 20 in Tokyo. It may
be too late to do anything about it, but here are the facts. At least
a repetition of such unfair business practice can be prevented.
Yours very truly,
AMERICAN PETROLEUM EXPORT COMPANY
/s/ Glean A. Smith
President
GAS:CCJ
P.S.--We also have Invitation to Bid #BT-49-5792 for 18,000 metric tons
of asphalt, the value of which is about $600,000. The same condition
applies and the same comments as above. This bid closes in Tokyo

=== Page 489 === (confidence 0.99)
Statement of General MaoArthur to
General MacArthur today announced unequivocally that
no devaluation of the Japanese yen is contemplated.
As a result of detailed studies of the devaluation
problem within this headquarters, the Supreme Commander
has determined that there is no basis for changing the
yen-dollar ration.
Authoritative sources in GHQ state that the decision
to clarify the yen devaluation issue was made by Goneral
MacArthur to terminate the uncertainty whioh has inhibi-
ted foreign commercial transactions since the announce-
mont of revaluation of sterling and other curreneies.
Competent observers believe that todays announeement
will permit the prompt full-scale resumption of trade
between Japan and its normal markets. Adjustments in
export prices required for specific commodities now quoted
at above world market levels will be accomplished through
the rationalization program and other measures now in
progress without requiring any change in currency valuss.

The Standard Braid and Produce Conpany of Japan was a Japanese
juridical firm wholly owned and controlled by persons of German
nationality, and, therefore, the assets of the company were impounded
and blocked in accordance with JCs 1380/15 and the stocks mentioned
above are now in the possession of GHQ, SCAP, pending determination
as to whether or not they come within the meaning of Allied. Control
Council Germany Law No. 5, October 30, 1945, as amended by Regulation
1, May 10, 1946.
At a subsequent date following screening of the ownership of
the Standard Braid and Produce Company, it was discovered that 8s of
the firm was owned by intereets which did not come within the purview
of the Allied Control Council Germany Law No. 5 and that only 15% of
the firm was subject to vesting. Consequently, the firm and its
assets have been released with the exception of the 15% vested
interest and the property belonging to Kyllman & Bauer of Bolivia.
Available information in GHQ, SCAP indicates that the firm of
Kyllman & Bauer was wholly owned and controllec by German interests
in Bolivia and that the property was subsequently vested and later
sold by the Bolivian Government to "Gumucio y Compania, Limitada".
It is, therefore, the opinion of the Civil Property Custodian
Section that any assets belonging to the firm of Kyllman & Bauer
outside the sovereign-ty of Bolivia should be vested within the
purview of Allied Con rol Council Germany Law No. 5, even though
the firm of Gumucio & Co., Ltd. acquired the assets of Kyllman &
Bauer from the Government of Bolivia before the end of hostilities
with Germany and thus refore the enactment of Allied Control Council
Germany Law No. 5.
Information received by this Mission from the American Embasmy,
La Paz, Bolivia (Operations Memorandum to US POLAD. dated January 26,
1949) revealed that Kyllman & Bauer was considered to be German and
was, therefore, vested; that the sale of Kyllman & Bauer to Gumucio &
Co., Ltd. was not ordered by the Bolivian Government but was
authorized
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 493 === (confidence 0.98)
Tokyo's Despatch No. 774,
November 7, 1949.
- 3 -
authorized by the "Junta de Defensa Economica de Bolivia" in a
resolution dated March 32. 1945; that, although the firm of
Gumucio & Co. was registered in the Nationel Chamber of Commerce
in September 1945 as Bolivian, the partnere of Guucio & Co. were
in fact German nationels. In the Embassy's view the transfer of
Kyllman & Co. to Gumucio & Co was obvioualy a closking operation
and, in spite of the registry of Gumucio & Co. as a Bolivian firm,
practically no effective change was made in its composition by the
transfer. On the recommendation of the Exbasey, therefore,
Gumucio & Co. was included on the Proclaimed List as had been
Kyllman & Beuer.
This Mission respectfully requests the Department's opinion
as to the applicability of Allied Control Council Germany Law No.
5. October 30. 1945, as amended by Regulation 1, May 10, 1946, to
the cleim of Gumucio & Co. gainet the Stancard Braid and Produce
Company of Japan.

My dear Dr. Benner:
Your letter of November 24, 1949 addressed to Mr. R. H. Whitman
with regard to the establishment of dollar credit in the United
States to pay for engineering information requested by Japanese mamm-
facturers, has been referred to me.
General MacArthur has announoed that the Japanese Goverament is
prepared to place export on a private basis as of December 1, 1949
S
and permit exporters to handle their transections direetly with
authorised banks abroad in accordance ith normal commeroial prae-
894.515//11-2449
tices. This action is to take place under the Foreign Exehange and
Trade Control Law which has been passed by the Japanese Diet on
30 November 1949. It has also been announeed that the Japanese Gov-
ernment in the not too distant future is to place imports on a pri-
vate basis. This would indicate that any arrangements that you may
make with Japanese manufacturers in the coming period would be handled
as a normal commercial transaction in view of the removal of the trade
and financial restrictions that prevailed prior to the passage of the
Foreign Exchange and Trade Control Law.
It is suggested that you inform your Japanese correspondents
that it will be necessary for them to arrange for the opening of a
letter of credit in your favor or otherwise acquire dollar funds with
which to pay for your services and that this would appear to be a mt
ter between themselves and the Japanese Foreign Exchange Control auther-
ities which would be governed by existing laws and regulations.

Foreign Exchange and Foreign Trade Control Law
Chapter I. General Provisions
Article 1.
The purpose of this Law is to provide for the. control of
foreign exchange, foreign trade and other foreign trans
actions, necessary for the proper development of foreign
trade and for the safeguarding of the balance of inter-
national payments and the stability of the currency, as
well as the most economic and beneficial use of foreign
currency funds, for the sake of the rehabilitation and
the expansion of the national economy.
Article 2.
The provisions of this Law and orders issued to implement
this Law shall be reviewed with the objective of gradually
relaxing and eliminating the restrictions established by.
this Law or the orders issued thereunder, as the need for
them subsides.
Article 3.
There shall be established under the cabinet a. Ministerial
Council which shall have as its responsibility the estab-
lishment of the foreign exchange budgets.
2. Necessary matters concerring the organization and
operation cf the Ministerial Council shall be provided for
by Cabinet Order.
Article 4.
There shall be established by separate Law an organization
titled the Foreign Exchange Control Board.
Article 5.
This Law shall apply alsc to acts, performed outside Japan
by representatives, agents, employees and other persons
engaged by juridical persons having their head offices or
main places of business in Japan, in regard to the pro-
perty or business of such juridical persons. The same
shall apply to acts. performed outside Japan by persons
domiciled in Japan, their representatives, employees and
other persons engaged by them, in regard to their pro-
perty or business.
Article 6. In order to make uniform the application of this Law and
orders issued in acccrdance therewith the following ter-
minology shall be defined to mean:
1。
"Japan" shall mean Japan Proper, Hokkaido, Shikoku,
Kyushu and dependent islands thereof as stipulated
by orders.
2.
"Foreign countries" shall mean territories outside
Japan,
3.
"National currency" shall mean Japanese currency.
4.
"Foreign currency" shall mean any other than national
currency.
5. rrighange

in Japar (agencies, establishments, etc.) of exchange

securities and documents in which claimable assets.
are embodied.
16.
"Property" shall mean property included under items
7, 10, 11, 13, 15 and any other property.
Article 7. The basic rate of exchange of the national currency shall
be unitary for all kinds of transactions and determined
by the Minister of Finance with approval of the Cabinet.
2. The Minister of Finance shall maintain orderly cross
rates of exchange with foreign currencies.
3. The Foreign Exchange Control Board shall determine
with approval of the Minister of Finance the rates
at which it will buy and sell foreign exchange.
40
The Fereign Exchange Control Board may wdth the ap-

basic exchange rate under Paragraph 1 or cross rates
under Paragraph 2 in the case of spot exchange trans-
actions by more than one (1) per cent.

When the Minister of Finance or the Foreign Exchange

cross rates, or the buying and selling rates of foreign
exchange or commissions related thereto, no person may
perform transactions not in accordance therewith.
Article 8. Transactions authorized under this Law may be effected only
with currencies prescribed by the Minister of Finance.
Article 9.
The competent minister may as provided for by Cabinet
Order, if deemed urgently necessary in case a sudden change
takes place in the economic situation, suspend transactions
governed by the provisions of this Law for a designated
pericd sperified in the Cabinet Order.
2. The suspension under the provisions of the preceding
paragraph shall nct result in default of payments under
provisions of this Law and the possible delay thereof
will be limited to the extent of the designated period
specified by Cabinet Order.

CHAPTER VI
Foreign Trade
Article 47. Export of goods from Japan will be permitted with
the minimum restrictions thereon consistent with
the purpose of this Law.
Article 48. 1. Any person desiring to export goods from Japan
may be required to obtain the approval of the
Minister of International Trade and Industry for
those types of export goods and/or method of
transactions or payments as provided for by
Cabinet Orders.
2. The restrictions provided for by the Cabinet
Orders specified in the preceding paragraph shall
be within the limit of necessity for the main-
tenance of the balance of International payment
and sound development of international trade or
national economy.
Article 49.
The Minister of International Trade and Industry
may by ordinane require from any person desiring
to export goods an adequate certification that
satisfactory payment is provided for by Cabinet
Order.
Article 50.
Any person exporting goods shall exercise due
respect to laws and regulations prohibiting
unfair competition in the country of final
destination of such goods.
Article 51.
The Minister of International Trade and Industry
may suspend by ordinance the shipment of export
goods, designating the articles and/or destination
for a period not exceeding one month. when he deems
it necessary as a matter of grave emergency.
Article 52.
In order to ensure the most economic and beneficial.
imports of goods within the ssope of foreign
exchange budget any person desiring to effect
import may be required to obtain approval therefor
as provided for by Cabinet Order.
Article 53,
The Minister of Intermational Trade and Industry
may prohibit any person who, in connection with
the export or import of goods, has violated the
provisions of this Law, ordinances or measures
based thereon, from engaging in import or export.
transactions for a period not exseeding one year.
Article 54.

CHAPTER VII
Appeals and Revmew
Article 56.
Any person aggrieved by the action or disposition
made by a :orpetent govertunent agency under the
previsions ef this Law or orders issued thereunder
may appeal tc such agency by filing a petition in
writing stating the substance of his grievance.
Article 57. 1. Any government agency re:eiving sueh petition
as mentioned in the preceding Artiole shall afford
to the petitioner an opportunity for public
hearing after reasonable advance notice.
2. The notice shall state the time. place, and
issues invelved.
3. At the hearing. oppertunity shall be afforded
the petitioner and all interested persons to present
evidence and argument with respect to said issue.
Article 58.
The competent government agency shall make the
decision on the issues. Such de:ision shall be
made in writing and copies thereof shall be
delivered to the petitioner and other interested
persone.
Article 59.
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 510 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure to Tokyo's
Despatch No: 819,
November 25, 1949.
UNCLASSIFIED
-14-
Article 59.
Appropriate rules of procedure for appeals, notice,
hearing and decision shall be provided for by
Cabinet Order.
Article 60.
Any person aggrieved by a final decision rendered
by any competent government agency in accordance
with the preceding Articles of this Chapter, shall
be entitled to judicial review thereof.
Article 61.
Suits against the decisions of the competent govern-
ment agencies under this law shall be under the
jurisdiction of the High Courts. Proceedings for
review shall be instituted by filing a petition in
such court within thirty days after the service of
the final de ision of the government agency. Copies
of the petition shall be served upon the government
agency and upon all. other interested persons
involved in the hearing befere the such ageney.
Article 62.
Within thirty days after the service of the petition,
or within such further time as he court may allow,
the government agency shall transmit to the
reviewing court the orginal or a certified copy
of the entire re: ord of the proceedings under review;
the record may be shortened by agreement of the
persens and government agencies involved in the
hearing.
Article 63.
The review shall be confined to the record, except
that in cases of alleged irregularities in procedure
before the government agen-y, not shuwn in the
record, testimony thereon may be taken by the court.
Article 64. The court may affirm the de ision of the government
agency or remand the case for furtner proceedings;
or it may reverse or modify the decision if the
substantial rights of the petitioners may have been
prejudiced because the administrative findings,
inferences, conclusion, or decisions are:
(1) in violation of constitutional provisions; or
(2) in excess of the statutory authority or
jurisdiction of the government agency; or
(4) affected by cther error of law; or
(5) unsupported by ompetent, material, and
substantial evidence, cr
(6) unwarranted by the fa: ts to the extent that
the facts are subjet to tria de novo by
the reviewing court.

CHAPTER VIII
Miscellaneous
Article 65. Nothing in the provisions of this Law shall-be.
construed to repeal, modify or affect application
of the Trade Association Law (Law No. 191 of 1948)
or the Law relating to Prohibition of Private
Monopoly and Method of Preserving Fair Trade (Law
No. 54 of 1947) or the power of the Fair Trade
Commission to take action thereunder in any
particular situation.
Article 66.
Those provisions of this Law or orders thereunder,
which stipulate that the license, approval, or
other actions of government agencies, or the
foreign exchange banks are required, shall not
apply, as may be provided for by Cabinet Order,
in case a government agency performs acts requiring
license, approval or other action concerned.
Article 67.
In addition to those reports provided for in this
Law, the competent government agencies may require,
within the limit of the necessity for ensuring
the enforcement of this Law, other reports from
any person who carries out transactions governed
by this Law or other persons concerned as provided
for by Cabinet Order.
Article 68.
The competent government agencies, may within the
limit of the necessity for ensuring the enfrrcement
of this Law, permit officials concerned to eucer
the places of business., or offices of the foreign
exchange banks, and money changers during hours
of business and inspect books, documents and
other articles, or interrogate the persons concerned.
2. In conducting spot inspections in accordance
with the provision of the preceding paragraph, a
certificate to show his position shall be carried
by the official and presented to the persons
concerned.
3. The authority of spot inspection or interroga-
tion in accordance with the provision of Paragraph 1
shall not be construed as being recognized for the
search of crimes.
Article 69. The competent government agencies may as provided
for by Cabinet Order require the Bank of Japan or
the foreign exchange banks to handle a part of
the business concerning the execution of this Law.
